\chapteropen[\href{https://www.hodinkee.com/articles/patek-philippe-caliber-89-easter-problem}{The astronomical clock in Notre-Dame-de-Strasbourg Cathedral, for computing the date of Easter.  Easter falls on the first Sunday after the first full moon on or after the nominal spring equinox of March~21. Calculation of this date was a great challenge for mechanisms of that time, 1843.}]{automata/pix/easterclock.jpg}{Automata} %https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:CIMA_mg_8332.jpg
% Use as much of http://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/14818/4731 as possible

Our touchstone model of computation is the Turing machine.
It has two components, the box and the tape.
In this chapter we will focus on the box:~we
we will consider what can be done with states alone, 
\citetext{what can be done by a machine having a number of possible configurations that is  bounded}{%
  From Rabin, Scott, Finite Automata and Their Decision Problems, 1959:
  \textit{Turing machines are widely considered to be the abstract prototype
  of digital computers; workers in the field, however, have felt more and more
  that the notion of a Turing machine is too general to serve as an accurate
  model of actual computers. It is well known that even for simple
  calculations it is impossible to give an a priori upper bound on the amount
  of tape a Turing machine will need for any given computation.
  It is precisely this feature that renders Turing's concept unrealistic.
  % 
  In the last few years the idea of a finite automaton has appeared in the
  literature. These are machines having only a finite number of internal
  states that can be used for memory and computation. The restriction on
  finiteness appears to give a better approximation to the idea of a physical
  machine. Of course, such machines cannot do as much as Turing machines,
  but the advantage of being able to compute an arbitrary general recursive
  function is questionable, since very few of these functions come up in
  practical applications.} % http://cstheory.stackexchange.com/a/14816
  }.\footnote{%
Studying the parts of the machine is natural but there is
another motivation. 
A person could object to Turing's model by observing that
% Turing machines are not the right model of what can be done by 
% an 
% \citetext{idealized discrete and deterministic device}{%
%   Turing machines are `idealized' in that, for instance, the tape
%   length is
%   not bounded by the number of elementary 
%   particles in the universe.} on the grounds that 
% they write to the unbounded tape.
there is a machine that iterates writing a character and then moving right, 
and thereby goes through unboundedly many configurations,
while no physical device can do that.
A rejoinder is that we for instance define a 
`book' to be pages with words
and don't worry whether physics limits the number of possible pages.
Happily, we don't need to go into this to justify 
our interest. 
Tapeless machines are quite practical, appearing
often in everyday computing, which is justification enough.}

  


\section{Finite State machines}\index{Finite State machine|(}
We produce a new model of computation, the Finite State machine, by modifying
the Turing machine definition.
We will strip out the capability to write, changing from
read/write to read-only.
It will turn out that these machines can do many things, but
not as many as Turing machines.

\subsection{Definition}
We begin with some examples.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:PowerSwitch}
This power switch has two states, $q_{\text{off}}$ and $q_{\text{on}}$, and
its input alphabet has one token, \str{toggle}.
(Its standard symbol is on the right.)
\begin{center}
  \newlength{\jhscratchheight}
  % \newsavebox{\scratchbox}
  \savebox{\scratchbox}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa00.pdf}}
  \settoheight{\jhscratchheight}{\usebox{\scratchbox}}
  \vcenteredhbox{\usebox{\scratchbox}}
  \hspace{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=0.65\jhscratchheight]{automata/pix/powerbutton.png}}  % Free, from: http://www.clker.com/clipart-9978.html
\end{center}
\end{example}

The state $q_{\text{on}}$ is drawn with a double circle,
denoting that it is a different kind of state than $q_{\text{off}}$.
Finite State machines 
can't write to the tape so they need some other way 
to declare the computation's outcome.
We say that $q_{\text{on}}$ is an 
\definend{accepting state}\index{accepting state}\index{final state}\index{state!accepting} 
or 
\definend{final state}.\index{final state}\index{state!final} 
A computation accepts its input string if it ends with the machine in an
accepting state.

\begin{example} \label{ex:Turnstile}
Operate the turnstile below by putting in two tokens and then pushing through.
It has three states and
its input alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{token},\str{push}}$.
As with Turing machines, the states here serve as a form of memory,
although a limited one.
For instance, $q_{\text{one}}$ is how the turnstile ``remembers'' that it
has so far received one token.
\begin{center}

  % \newsavebox{\scratchbox}

  \vcenteredhbox{\usebox{\scratchbox}}

\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:VendingMachine}
This vending machine dispenses items that cost $30$~cents.\footnotemark[1]
The picture is complex so we will show it in three layers.
First are the arrows for nickels.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa02.pdf}
\end{center}
After receiving $30$~cents and getting another nickel,
this machine does something not very sensible:~it stays in $q_{30}$.
In practice a machine would have further states to keep track of
overages so that it could give change but here we ignore that.
Next comes the arrows for dimes
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa03.pdf}
\end{center}
and for quarters.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa04.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}\footnotetext[1]{%
  US coins are:~$1$~cent coins not used here,
  nickels are $5$~cents, dimes are $10$~cents, and quarters are $25$.}

\begin{example} \label{ex:ModuloFourFSM}
This machine's alphabet is the set of bits, $\B=\set{\str{0},\str{1}}$.
As an example, if the input string is $\sigma=\str{101101}$ then the machine 
reads those bits, first passing from $q_0$ 
into $q_1$ and $q_1$ again, then through $q_2$ and $q_3$ and $q_3$ again,
before finally returning to~$q_0$.
As $q_0$ has a double circle, the machine accepts~$\sigma$.
\begin{center}    
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa06.pdf}
\end{center}
This machine accepts a bitstring if 
the number of
\str{1}'s in its input is a multiple of four.
\end{example}

We defined Turing machines as sets of instructions.
Instructions have the advantage of being intuitive, and of matching how
we think about CPU's on everyday computers.
While we have later occasionally referred to instructions,
what has mattered most is that
they describe the machine's next-state function,~$\Delta$.
For the definition of Finite State machines we will cut right to giving 
it in terms of the next-state function.

%<*def:FSM>
\begin{definition} \label{def:FiniteStateMachine}
A \definend{Finite State machine}\index{Finite State machine}
or
\definend{Finite State automata}\index{Finite State automata|see{Finite State machine}} 
$\sequence{Q,q_{0},F,\Sigma,\Delta}$
has a finite \definend{set of states}~$Q$,\index{state}\index{Finite State machine!state}
one of which is the \definend{start state}~$q_{0}$,\index{start state}
a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of 
\definend{final states}\index{final state}\index{state!final}\index{Finite State machine!final state}
or
\definend{accepting states},\index{accepting state}\index{state!accepting}\index{Finite State machine!accepting state} 
a finite \definend{input alphabet}\index{alphabet!input}\index{input alphabet}\index{Finite State machine!alphabet} 
set~$\Sigma$,
and a 
\definend{next-state function}\index{next-state function}\index{function!next-state}\index{Finite State machine!next-state function}
or \citetext{\definend{transition function} 
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{Q}$}{%
  Some authors allow the transition function to be partial.
  That is, some authors allow that for some state-symbol pairs
  there is no next state.
  This choice by an author is a matter of convenience, as for any such
  machine you can create an \definend{error state}\index{state!error}\index{error state}
  $q_{\text{error}}$ 
  or \definend{dead state},\index{dead state}\index{state!dead}
  that is not an accepting state and that transitions only to itself, 
  and send all such pairs there.
  This transition function is total, and the new machine has the same
  collection of accepted strings as the old.}.\index{transition function}\index{function!transition}\index{Finite State machine!transition function}
\end{definition}
%</def:FSM>

A full description of the action of these machines comes
after a few more examples.
But basically, to work a machine, load a
string input on the tape and press \texttt{Start}.\index{Start button@\texttt{Start} button}
At each step the machine consumes one tape token:~it reads, acts on,
and deletes it, and then moves so that the head points to the next 
tape cell.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{asy/share/machine02.pdf}
\end{center}
The picture shows a light labeled `Accept'. 
When the machine stops, when the input string is fully consumed, if 
the current state is
an accepting state then the light comes on.
In this case we say that the machine 
\definend{accepts}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by deterministic Finite State machine} 
the input string, otherwise it 
\definend{rejects}\index{rejects}\index{string rejected}
that string.

Here is a trace of the steps when
\cref{ex:ModuloFourFSM}'s modulo~$4$ machine is started with
the input string $\tau=\str{10110}$ on the tape.
Since $q_3$ is not an accepting state the machine rejects~$\tau$ .
\begin{center}
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises modfour000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises modfour001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises modfour002.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    3 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises modfour003.pdf} \\
    4 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises modfour004.pdf} \\
    5 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises modfour005.pdf} 
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{center}
These 
machines consume one character per step and stop once all the characters are 
gone so they are sure to halt.  
There is no \HP{} for Finite State machines.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}
The machine below accepts a string if and only if it
contains at least two \str{0}'s as well as an even number of \str{1}'s.
(In tables we denote accepting states 
with `\acceptingstatemark', as here next to 
$q_2$).\index{accepting state!in transition tables}\index{final state!in transition tables}\index{+@$+$!in transition tables}
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa11.pdf}}               
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{0}    &\str{1}   \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_3$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_4$    \\
    \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$ &$q_5$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_0$    \\
    $q_4$ &$q_5$ &$q_1$    \\
    $q_5$ &$q_5$ &$q_2$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
We take this chance to describe
the key to designing Finite State machines. 
Each state should have an 
intuitive function that is directed to the machine's purpose, and that is
unique.
The drawing from that example brings this out.
Its top row has states where the machine has so far seen an even number of
\str{1}'s (possibly zero many), 
while its states in the bottom row have seen an odd number.
Its first column holds states where the machine has seen no \str{0}'s,
its second column holds states where there has been one,
and its third has states where there have been two.
Thus we can articulate that $q_4$ means, 
``so far the machine has seen one~\str{0} and an
odd number of \str{1}'s,''
while $q_5$ means, ``so far the machine has
seen two \str{0}'s but an odd number of \str{1}'s.''
\end{remark}

Our Finite State machine descriptions often take
the alphabet to be clear from the context.
Thus, \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}'s alphabet is
$\B=\set{\str{0},\str{1}}$. 
For in-practice machines, the alphabet is the
set of characters that the machine could conceivably receive, so that
a text-handling routine built to modern standards 
might well accept all of \citetext{Unicode}{%
  While in the early days of computers
  characters could be encoded with standards such as ASCII, which includes only
  upper and lower case unaccented letters, digits,
  a few punctuation marks, and a few
  control characters,
  today's global interconnected world needs more.
  The Unicode standard assigns a unique number called a code point to
  every character in every language (to a fair approximation).
  See \autocite{wiki:Unicode}.\index{Unicode}}.\index{Unicode}
But for the examples and exercises in this book 
we will use small alphabets.\footnote{%
  We often use \protect\str{a}, \protect\str{b}, \protect\str{c}, etc., 
  because something like 
  `$\str{c}^2$' is clearer than
  `$\str{0}^2$'.}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSAValidIntegers}
%<*ex:FSAValidIntegers>
This machine
accepts strings that are valid decimal representations of integers.
So it accepts the strings \str{21} and \str{-7} and  \str{+37} 
but does not accept \str{501-}.

The transition graph and the table both group some inputs together when
they result in the same action.
For instance, when in state~$q_0$ this machine does the same thing
whether the input is \str{+} or \str{-}, namely it passes into
$q_1$.
%</ex:FSAValidIntegers>
\begin{center} 
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa07.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{+}, \str{-} &\str{0}, \ldots{} \str{9} &\tabulartext{--other--}   \\
    \cline{2-4}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    $q_1$ &$e$   &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    \acceptingstate $q_2$ &$e$   &$q_2$ &$e$   \\
    $e$   &$e$   &$e$   &$e$   \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
Any wrong input character sends the machine to the state~$e$.
Finite State machines often have an \definend{error state},\index{error state}
which is a sink in that once the machine enters that state
then it never leaves.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}
This machine accepts strings that are members of the set
$\set{\str{jpg},\str{pdf},\str{png}}$.
It is our first example with more than one accepting state.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa09.pdf}}
\end{center}
That drawing omits many edges, the ones involving the error state~$e$.
For instance, from state~$q_0$ any input character other than \str{j}
or~\str{p} is an error.
% The drawing below shows the extra edges for the top branch; putting in all the
% edges would make a mess.
We omit all of these edges because they would make the drawing hard to read.
This illustrates that while pictures are better for simple machines,
past some point of complexity, 
a transition table presentation is better than a picture.
\end{example}

% \urldef\goldbachurl\url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goldbach%27s_conjecture}
That example points out that  
\citetext{if a language is finite then there is a Finite State machine that accepts
a string if and only if it is a member of that language}{%
  In practice the suggestion that for any finite set of strings there is a 
  Finite State machine that accepts it, simply by listing
  all of the cases, may not be reasonable.
  For example,
  there are finitely many people and each has finitely many
  active phone numbers so
  the set of all currently-active phone numbers
  is a finite language. 
  But constructing a machine for it
  would be silly.
  In addition, a finite language doesn't have to be large for it to be
  difficult, in a sense.
  Take
  \definend{Goldbach's conjecture},
  that every even number greater
  than~$2$ is the sum of
  two primes, as in $4=2+2$, $6=3+3$, $8=3+5$, \ldots{}
  % (see \goldbachurl).
  Computer testing shows that this pattern continues to hold up to very large
  numbers but no one knows if it is true for all evens.
  Now consider the set consisting of
  the string~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots\,\str{9}}}$
  representing in decimal the smallest even number
  that is not the sum of two primes.
  This set is finite since it has either
  one member or none.
  But while that set is tiny, we don't know what it contains.
  }.

\begin{example} \label{ex:FSAMultOfThree}
Finite State machines can
accomplish reasonably hard tasks.
This one accepts strings representing natural numbers
that are multiples of three such as \str{15} and~\str{8013},
and does not accept non-multiples such as \str{14} and~\str{8012}.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa12.pdf}}
\end{center}
This machine accepts the empty string.
\Cref{exer:FSAModifyMultOFThreeMachine} asks for a modification 
to accept only non-empty strings.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
In the 1940's, phone call connections were handled by 
\citetext{simple devices}{%
  The devices to do the switching were invented in 1889 by an undertaker whose
  competitor's wife was the local telephone operator and routed calls to
  her husband's business.
  \autocite{wiki:Strowger}}
for local calls but required operator intervention for long distance.
That changed with the adoption of the Finite State machine here,
which 
\citetext{allowed users to directly dial long distance in North America}{%
  See the description of the North America Numbering Plan
  \autocite{wiki:NANumbering}.}.
Consider dialing \str{1-802-555-0101}.
The initial \str{1} means that 
the call leaves the local office.
The \str{802} is an area code; the system can tell that this is not a 
\citetext{same-area local exchange}{%
  Initially, large states, those divided into multiple numbering plan areas, 
  were assigned area codes with a \str{1} in the second position. 
  Areas that covered entire states or provinces got codes with 
  \str{0} as the middle digit. 
  That was abandoned by the early 1950's.
  \autocite{wiki:NANumbering}.}
because
its second digit is~\str{0} or~\str{1}.
Next, the \str{555}
routes the call to a local office.
Then that office's device makes the connection to line \str{0101}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa13.pdf}
\end{center}
Today, no longer are area codes required to
have a middle digit of \str{0} or~\str{1}.
This additional flexibility is possible because
switching now happens entirely in software.
\end{example}

After the definition of Turing machine we gave a complete
description of the action of those machines.
We now do the same for Finite State machines.\label{page:DefnYieldsFiniteStateMachine}
%<*discussion:FSMConfiguration>
A 
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{Finite State machine!configuration} 
of a Finite State machine is 
a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}$ where
$q$ is a state, $q\in Q$, and $\tau$ is a (possibly empty) 
string,~$\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
A machine starts in an 
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{Finite State machine!initial configuration}
$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0}$,
so that $\tau_0$ is the
\definend{input}\index{input string}\index{Finite State machine!input string}
and $q_0$ is the \definend{initial state}.\index{initial state}\index{Finite State machine!initial state}

A Finite State machine acts by making a sequence of \definend{transitions}    
between configurations.
A machine's configuration after the $s$-th transition (for $s\in\N^+$) 
is its configuration at 
\definend{step~$s$}.\index{step of a computation}\index{Finite State machine!step} 
Fitting with $\mathcal{C}_0$, we write $\mathcal{C}_1$, $\mathcal{C}_2$,
and so forth.

Here is the rule for making one transition.
% (we may say for emphasis that it is an 
% \definend{allowed} or \definend{legal} transition).
Begin with $\mathcal{C}_s=\sequence{q,\tau_s}$.
Either $\tau_s$ is empty or it is not.
If $\tau_s$ is not empty then
pop its leading character~$c$.
That is, because $\tau_s$ is not empty it decomposes into
a first character and a remaining string, $\tau_s=c\concat\tau_{s+1}$.
Then 
the machine's next state is $\hat{q}=\Delta(q,c)$ and its next
configuration is $\mathcal{C}_{s+1}=\sequence{\hat{q},\tau_{s+1}}$.
We denote this before-after relationship between configurations
with $\mathcal{C}_s\yields\mathcal{C}_{s+1}$.\footnote{%
  As earlier, read $\yields$ aloud 
  as ``yields.''}\index{yields@$\yields$, yields!for Finite State machines}

The other possibility is that~$\tau_s$ is the empty string.
Then $\mathcal{C}_s$ is a 
\definend{halting configuration}.\index{halting configuration}\index{configuration!halting}\index{Finite State machine!halting configuration} 
No transitions follow.
Every Finite State machine eventually reaches a halting configuration
because at each transition the tape string loses a character.
A 
\definend{computation}\index{computation!Finite State machine}\index{Finite State machine!computation}
for a Finite State machine is a sequence 
$
  \mathcal{C}_0 \yields \mathcal{C}_1 % \yields \mathcal{C}_2
   \yields \cdots\, \mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q,\emptystring}
$,
which 
we abbreviate
$\mathcal{C}_0\mathrel{\kleenestar{\yields}}\mathcal{C}_h$.\footnote{%
  Read $\yields^*$ as ``yields eventually.''}\index{yields@$\yields$, yields!for Finite State machines}
If $\mathcal{C}_h$'s state is a final state, $q\in F$, 
then the machine \definend{accepts}\index{accept an input}\index{Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by deterministic Finite State machine} 
the input string~$\tau_0$. 
Otherwise it 
\definend{rejects}~$\tau_0$.\index{reject an input}\index{Finite State machine!reject string}
%</discussion:FSMConfiguration>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:MultipleOfThreeFormal}
The multiple of three machine of \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} with input \str{8013}
gives the computation
$
  \sequence{q_0,\str{8013}}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,013}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,13}
  \yields\sequence{q_0,3}
  \yields\sequence{q_0,\emptystring}
$.
Since $q_0$ is accepting, the machine accepts \str{5013}.
\end{example}

%<*def:FSMLanguage>
\begin{definition}
The set of strings accepted by a Finite State machine~$\FSM$ is the
language
\definend{recognized},\index{recognized language!of a Finite State machine}\index{language!recognized by a Finite State machine}
or \definend{decided},\index{decided language}\index{language!decided by a Finite State machine}
% or \definend{accepted},
by the machine,\footnotemark[3]\index{accepted language|see{recognized}}\index{language!accepted by a Finite State machine|see{language, recognized by a Finite State machine}}
or just simply the
\definend{language of that machine,~$\lang(\FSM)$}.\index{language!of a Finite State machine}\index{Finite State machine!language of}
\end{definition}\footnotetext[3]{%
  Finite State machines must halt
  and so there is no notion like computably enumerable.
  Thus the languages that such a machines can decide is the same as
  the languages that it can recognize
  (in contrast with the case for Turing machines, as 
  defined on page~\protect\pageref{df:TMDecidesLanguage}).
  For these machines, `recognized' is the more common term.}
%</def:FSMLanguage>

\begin{example} 
The language of the power switch machine from \cref{ex:PowerSwitch} is the set
of strings consisting of the token \str{toggle} given an even number of times,
$\lang=\set{\str{toggle}^{2k}\suchthat k\in\N}$.  
The language of the turnstile machine, \cref{ex:Turnstile} is the set
of strings from the alphabet
$\Sigma=\set{\str{token},\str{push}}$ of the form
\begin{equation*}
  (\str{push})^{k_0}\,\str{token}\,(\str{push})^{k_1}\,(\str{token})^{k_2}
    \,\bigl(\,(\str{push})^{k_3}\,\str{token}\,(\str{push})^{k_4}\,(\str{token})^{k_5}\,\bigr)^n
\end{equation*}
for $k_2,k_3,k_5\in\N^+$ and $n, k_0,k_1,k_4\in\N$.
\end{example}

%<*def:ExtendedTransitionFcn>
\begin{definition} \label{def:ExtendedTransitionFcn}
  For any Finite State machine,
  the 
  \definend{extended transition function}\index{extended transition function}\index{transition function!extended}\index{function!extended transition} 
  $\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{Q}$
  gives the state in which the machine ends after starting in the
  start state and consuming the given string.
\end{definition}
%</def:ExtendedTransitionFcn>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:ExtendedTransFcn}
  Consider this machine and its transition function.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa15.pdf}}
    \hspace{2em}
    \begin{transitionfunction}
             &\str{a}  &\str{b}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate
      $q_1$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
      $q_2$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
  Its extended transition function $\extended{\Delta}$
  extends $\Delta$ 
  in that it repeats the first row of $\Delta$'s table.
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{a})=q_1
    \qquad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{b})=q_0
  \end{equation*}
  (We disregard the difference between $\Delta$'s input of characters 
  and $\extended{\Delta}$'s input of length~one strings.)
  This is $\extended{\Delta}$ on the length~two strings.
  \begin{equation*}
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{aa})=q_1
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{ab})=q_2
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{ba})=q_1
    \quad
    \extended{\Delta}(\str{bb})=q_0
  \end{equation*}
Observe that a string $\sigma$ is accepted 
by the machine if and only if
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$ is an accepting state.
For instance, the string \str{aa} is in the language of this machine
as $\extended{\Delta}(\str{aa})=q_1$, which is accepting.
\end{example}

We can give a constructive definition of $\extended{\Delta}$.
Fix a Finite State machine $\FSM$ with
transition function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{Q}$.
Begin with $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\set{q_0}$.
Then for $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and $t\in\Sigma$, define
$\extended{\Delta}(\tau\concat t)$ to be
$\Delta(q,t)$,
where $\extended{\Delta}(\tau)=q$. 

One way in which $\extended{\Delta}$ is handy is that it gives us
a nice alternative definition of when an input string is accepted 
by a machine:~$\tau_0$ is accepted if $\extended{\Delta}(\tau_0)\in F$.

This brings us back to determinism
because $\extended{\Delta}$ would not be well-defined
without it:~by determinism $\Delta$ has one next state
for all input configurations and so, by induction,
$\extended{\Delta}$ has one and only one output state for all input strings.

% Finally, note the similarity between $\extended{\Delta}$
% and $\phi_e$, the function computed by the Turing machine $\TM_e$.
% Both take as input the contents of their machine's start tape,
% and both give as output the result of multiple steps.

% \begin{TODO}
%   Add exercise that starts with a TM using only finite scratch memory, and
%   then produces an FSA doing that job.
% \end{TODO}


% ................................................
\begin{exercises}[For the exercises that give a language description,
a useful practice 
is to think through that description by naming five strings that 
are in the language and five that are not.]

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Using this machine, trace through the computation when the input is
\begin{exparts*}
\item \str{abba}
\item \str{bab}
\item \str{bbaabbaa}.
\end{exparts*}
Does the machine accept the string?
\begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa15.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
\begin{display}
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinai000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinai001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinai002.pdf} \\

\end{tapesequence}

Because $q_1$ is an accepting state, the machine accepts this string.
\item
\begin{display}
\begin{tapesequence}

    0 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaii000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaii001.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    2 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaii002.pdf} \\
    3 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaii003.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
\end{tapesequence}
\end{display}
The machine does not accept this string because $q_2$ is
not an accepting state. 
\item
\begin{display}
\begin{tapesequence}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    0 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaiii000.pdf} \\
    1 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaiii001.pdf} \\
    2 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaiii002.pdf} \\
  \end{tapesequencetable}
  \hspace*{2em}
  \begin{tapesequencetable}
    3 &\tapegraphic{\fsmexercises endsinaiii003.pdf} \\

\begin{exercise}
True or false:~because a Finite State machine is finite, its language must be
finite.
\begin{answer}\verified
  False.
  For instance, \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers}'s machine recognizes the
  language consisting of valid decimal representations of integers and
  thus containing each of these: \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{2}, \ldots
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your classmate says, ``I have a language~$\lang$ that recognizes
the empty string~$\emptystring$.''
Explain to them the mistake.
\begin{answer}\verified
  Languages do not recognize strings, machines recognize strings.
  Maybe they mean that they have a language that contains the empty string?
  Or maybe they mean that what they have 
  is the language of a machine, and this machine accepts the empty string?
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Rebut ``no Finite State machine can recognize the language
$\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$
because $n$ is infinite.''
\begin{answer}\verified
  First, ``$n$ is infinite'' is wrong.
  That set is a language $\set{\str{b}, \str{ab}, \str{a}^2\str{b}, \ldots}$
  and in each string from that language the number of \str{a}'s is finite.

  Second, there certainly is a machine that recognizes that language.
  This Finite State machine will do.
  \begin{center}  \small
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa31.pdf}}
  \end{center}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} 
How many transitions does an input string of length~$n$ cause 
a Finite State machine to undergo?  
$n$~many?  $n+1$?  $n-1$?
How many (not necessarily distinct) states will the machine have visited
after consuming the string?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Each input character consumed causes one transition.
  So an input string of length~$n$ causes the machine to undergo~$n$
  transitions.
  (This includes that the empty string~\emptystring{} causes no transitions.)
  With~$n$ transitions, the machine will have visited~$n+1$ many
  not necessarily distinct states, including the start state.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each of these descriptions of a language, give a 
one or two sentence informal English-language description.
Also list 
five strings that are elements as well as five that are not,
if there are that many. 
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang=\set{\alpha\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               \text{$\alpha=\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^n$ for $n\in\N$}}$
\item $\set{\beta\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               \text{$\beta=\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^m$ for $n,m\in\N$}}$
\item $\set{\str{b}\str{a}^n\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               n\in\N}$
\item $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^{n+2}\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
            \suchthat n\in\N}$
\item $\set{\gamma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
               \text{$\gamma$ has the form $\gamma=\alpha\concat\alpha$
                     for $\alpha\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item A string is in this language when it
    consists of two strings of \str{a}'s of equal length,
    separated by a single \str{b}.
    Five elements of the language are 
    \str{aba}, \str{aabaa}, \str{b}, \str{aaabaaa}, and \str{aaaabaaaa}.
    Five non-elements are \str{ba}, \str{aaba}, \str{a}, \str{abba},
    and~\str{bbabb}.
  \item The strings in this language have some number of \str{a}'s followed
    by a single~\str{b}, followed by a number of~\str{a}'s.
    The two numbers of~\str{a}'s need not be the same, and each may be zero.
    Five language elements are \str{abaa}, \str{aabaa}, \str{ab}, \str{baaaa},
    and~\str{b}.
    Five non-elements are \str{bab}, \str{aabb}, \str{bb}, \str{aabaab}, 
    and~\str{a}.
  \item The criteria for a string to be in this language is that it must
    start with~\str{b} and be followed by some number of~\str{a}'s.
    That could be zero-many \str{a}'s.
    Five elements of the language are \str{baa}, \str{ba}, \str{b},
    $\str{b}\str{a}^3$, and~$\str{b}\str{a}^4\!$.
    Five non-elements are \str{a}, \emptystring, \str{abaaa}, \str{baaab},
    and~\str{bb}. 
  \item A string is in this language if and only if it consists of some number
    of \str{a}'s followed by a~\str{b}, followed by some number of~\str{a}'s,
    where the second string of~\str{a}'s has two more than the first string.
    Five members of the language are \str{abaaa}, \str{aabaaaa},
    $\str{a}^3\str{b}\str{a}^5$, $\str{a}^4\str{b}\str{a}^6$, and~\str{baa}.
    Five non-members are \str{aba}, \str{abaaaa}, \str{ba}, \str{b},
    and~\str{bab}.
  \item This language has members that are doubles in that a member consists
    of the concatenation of two copies of some string.
    Five members of the language are \emptystring, \str{babbab}, \str{aa},
    \str{bbbbabbbba}, and~\str{abbaabba}.
    Five non-members are \str{aba}, \str{b}, \str{aaabaab}, \str{babaa}, 
    and~\str{bbb}. 
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % Models of Computation, Taylor p 509
For the machines of \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}, \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers}, 
\cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions}, and \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree},
answer these.
\begin{exparts*}
\item What are the accepting states?  
\item Does it accept the empty string~$\emptystring$?
\item What is the shortest string that each accepts?
\item Is the language of accepted strings infinite?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
    For~\cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} the only accepting state is $q_2$.
    In~\cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} also, the only accepting state is $q_2$.
    For \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} the accepting states 
    are $q_3$, $q_6$, and $q_8$.
    For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} it is $q_0$.
  \item
    Only \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} accepts the empty string~$\emptystring$ 
    because only in this machine is $q_0$ an accepting state.
  \item
    For \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} the shortest accepted string 
    is the length two string~\str{00}.
    For \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} it is any single-digit length one string,
    such as \str{6}.
    For \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} any of the length three strings
    \str{jpg}, \str{png}, and~\str{pdf}.
    For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} it is the empty string~$\emptystring$.
  \item
    The language of \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs} is the set of strings
    containing at least two \str{0}'s and an even number of \str{1}'s.
    Tha language is infinite because it contains, among other strings,
    \emptystring, \str{00},  \str{0000}, $\str{0}^6$, etc.
    The language of \cref{ex:FSAValidIntegers} is infinite because it 
    contains, among other strings, these: \str{0}, \str{1}, \str{2}, 
    \ldots\sentencespace
    The language of \cref{ex:FSAGraphicalFileExtensions} contains exactly
    three elements, 
    $\lang=\set{\str{jpg}, \str{png}, and~\str{pdf}}$.
    For \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree}, the language is infinite because it
    contains \str{0}, \str{3}, \str{6}, \ldots
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As in \cref{ex:MultipleOfThreeFormal}, give the computation for
the multiple of three machine with the initial string
\str{2332}.
\begin{answer}\verified
This is the computation.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{2332}}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,332}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,32}
  \yields\sequence{q_2,2}
  \yields\sequence{q_1,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
Because $q_1$ is not an accepting state,
the machine rejects \str{2332}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FSAModifyMultOFThreeMachine}
Modify the machine of \cref{ex:FSAMultOfThree} 
so that it accepts only non-empty strings.
\begin{answer}\verified
  We must change the machine so that its initial state is not an accepting state.
  \begin{center}  \small
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa22.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Recall the section's design advice that
  each state should have an intuitive function that is directed to the
  machine’s purpose, and that is unique.
  In that machine there are two states, $q_0$ and~$q_3$, 
  whose intuitive meaning is that the
  sum of the digits seen so far is congruent to $0$ modulo~$3$.
  However, they are different because the intial state $q_0$
  is not accepting.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce the transition graph picturing this transition
function.
What is the machine's language?
\begin{center}
  \small
  \begin{transitionfunction}
           &\str{a} &\str{b}    \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$  &$q_2$   &$q_1$       \\
    \acceptingstate $q_1$  &$q_0$   &$q_2$       \\
    $q_2$  &$q_2$   &$q_2$           
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  This is the graph picture.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa40.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The language is
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
         \text{$\sigma=\str{b}(\str{ab})^n$ for $n\in\N$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What language is recognized by this machine?
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa14.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The language is the set of strings over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$
  that contain exactly one~\str{b}.
  Restated, $\lang(\FSM)=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\str{a}^m\suchthat n,m\in\N}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each language, name five strings in the language and five that are not 
(if there are not five, name as many as there are).
Then produce a Finite State machine that recognizes that language.
Give both a circle diagram and a transition function table.
The alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\lang_1=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
               \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{a} and at least one \str{b}}}$
\item
$\lang_2=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
               \text{$\sigma$ has fewer than three \str{a}'s}}$
\item
$\lang_3=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
               \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{ab}}}$
\item
$\lang_4=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
               n,m\geq 2}$
\item
$\lang_5=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^m\str{a}^p\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
               \text{$m=2$ and $a, p\in\N$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
In the section body is the advice to design machines by 
describing an intuitive meaning for each state.
Although the question does not ask for
it, for each item here we give those. 
\begin{exparts}
\item
Five members are \str{ab}, \str{baab}, \str{ba}, 
$\str{b}^7\str{a}^3$, and $\str{a}\str{b}^6\str{a}$.
Five nonmembers are 
\str{aaaa}, \str{bbbb}, \str{a}, \emptystring, and \str{b}.
We take the meaning of the states to be as here.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|l}
  \textit{State}  &\textit{Description}  \\
  \hline
  $q_0$  &start state  \\  
  $q_1$  &have seen at least one \str{a}, no \str{b}'s   \\
  $q_2$  &have seen at least one \str{b}, no \str{a}'s   \\
  $q_3$  &at least one of each  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The circle drawing and transition table are \figureref{fig:AtLeastOneAOneB}.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa53.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
     \cline{2-3} 
     $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
     $q_1$  &$q_1$  &$q_3$  \\
     $q_2$  &$q_3$  &$q_2$  \\
    +$q_3$  &$q_3$  &$q_3$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Finite State machine accepting strings with at least one \str{a} and \str{b}.}
  \label{fig:AtLeastOneAOneB}
\end{figure}

\item
Five members are: \str{bb}, \emptystring, \str{aba}, \str{a}, 
and $\str{bbb}\str{aa}\str{b}$.
Five nonmembers are 
\str{aaa}, \str{baaa}, $\str{a}^5$, \str{bababa}, and $\str{a}^{10}\!$.
We take the states with this meaning.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|l}
  \textit{State}  &\textit{Description}  \\
  \hline
  $q_0$  &start state, no \str{a}'s seen yet  \\  
  $q_1$  &have seen one \str{a}, and maybe some number of \str{b}'s   \\
  $q_2$  &have seen two \str{a}'s   \\
  $q_3$  &have seen three or more \str{a}'s  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the circle diagram and transition table, see \figureref{fig:FewerThanThreeAs}.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa54.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
     \cline{2-3} 
    +$q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\
    +$q_1$  &$q_2$  &$q_1$  \\
    +$q_2$  &$q_3$  &$q_2$  \\
     $q_3$  &$q_3$  &$q_3$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Finite State machine accepting strings with fewer than three \str{a}'s.}
  \label{fig:FewerThanThreeAs}
\end{figure}

\item
Five members are \str{ab}, \str{bab}, \str{aab}, \str{baab}, 
and \str{abab}.
Five nonmembers: 
\str{a}, \str{bbb}, \emptystring, \str{b}, and \str{abababb}.
We use the states with this meaning.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|l}
  \textit{State}  &\textit{Description}  \\
  \hline
  $q_0$  &start state, have not just seen an \str{a}  \\  
  $q_1$  &have seen an \str{a}, waiting for a \str{b}   \\
  $q_2$  &have just seen \str{ab}   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The transition diagram and table are \figureref{fig:EndsInAB}.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa55.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
     \cline{2-3} 
     $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\
     $q_1$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
    +$q_2$  &$q_1$  &$q_0$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Finite State machine accepting strings that end in \str{ab}.}
  \label{fig:EndsInAB}
\end{figure}

\item
Five members are \str{aabb}, \str{aaabb}, \str{aabbb}, $\str{a}^7\str{b}^9\!$,  
and $\str{a}^2\str{b}^{200}\!$.
Five nonmembers are
\emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ab}, \str{ba}, and~\str{b}.
We use the states with this meaning.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|l}
  \textit{State}  &\textit{Description}  \\
  \hline
  $q_0$  &start state, have not seen any input  \\  
  $q_1$  &have seen one character, an \str{a}   \\
  $q_2$  &have seen at least two \str{a}'s and no \str{b}'s  \\
  $q_3$  &have seen at least two \str{a}'s and one \str{b}  \\
  $q_4$  &have seen at least two \str{a}'s and at least two \str{b}'s   \\
  $q_5=e$&error, cannot recover   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
See \figureref{fig:AtLeastTwoAsThenAtLeastTwoBs} 
for the circle diagram and the table.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa56.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
     \cline{2-3} 
     $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$e$  \\
     $q_1$  &$q_2$  &$e$  \\
     $q_2$  &$q_2$  &$q_3$  \\
     $q_3$  &$e$    &$q_4$  \\
    +$q_4$  &$e$    &$q_4$  \\
     $e$    &$e$    &$e$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Finite State machine for strings of at least two \str{a}'s and then at least two \str{b}'s.}
  \label{fig:AtLeastTwoAsThenAtLeastTwoBs}
\end{figure}

\item
Five members are \str{aabba}, \str{aabb}, \str{abb}, \str{bb},  
and $\str{a}^5\str{bb}^{7}\!$.
Five nonmembers are
\emptystring, \str{bab}, \str{aabaa}, \str{aabbbaa}, and~\str{aabbaab}.
We take the states with this meaning.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|l}
  \textit{State}  &\textit{Description}  \\
  \hline
  $q_0$  &have so far seen some number of \str{a}'s (including zero-many)  \\  
  $q_1$  &have seen some \str{a}'s followed by one \str{b}   \\
  $q_2$  &seen some \str{a}'s, two \str{b}'s, and some \str{a}'s (including zero-many)   \\
  $e$    &error   \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The two representations of the transition function are 
\figureref{fig:SomeAsTwoBsSomeAs}.
\begin{figure}
  \centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa57.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\Delta$} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}} 
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}   \\
     \cline{2-3} 
     $q_0$  &$q_0$  &$q_1$  \\
     $q_1$  &$e$    &$q_2$  \\
    +$q_2$  &$q_2$  &$e$  \\
     $e$    &$e$    &$e$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  \caption{Finite State machine for strings with some \str{a}'s, then two \str{b}'s, then some \str{a}'s.}
  \label{fig:SomeAsTwoBsSomeAs}
\end{figure}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Consider the language of strings over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$ containing
at least two \str{a}'s and at least two \str{b}'s.
Name five elements of the language and five non-elements,
if there are that many.
Then produce a Finite State machine recognizing this language.
As in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}, briefly describe the 
intuitive meaning of the states.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The introduction to these exercises suggests thinking through the language
  description by naming five strings that are in the language and five that
  are not. 
  The question doesn't ask for that, but as lagniappe here are some:~five
  elements of the language are 
  \str{aabb}, \str{bbaa}, \str{abab}, \str{aababb}, and~$\str{a}^3\str{b}^4\!$.
  Five non-elements are 
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aab}, and~\str{bbbbabbbb}.

  Here is the machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa30.pdf}}
  \end{center}

  The intuitive meaning of the states in the first row,
  $q_0$, $q_1$, and~$q_2$, is that 
  when the machine is in those states it has so far seen zero-many \str{b}'s.
  States in the second row are where the machine has so far seen
  one~\str{b}.
  The third row contains the states where the machine has seen two \str{b}'s 
  (or more).
  Similarly, the first column contains the states where the machine 
  has so far seen no \str{a}'s, in the second column are states 
  where the machine
  has seen a single~\str{a}, and in the final column the machine has
  see two or more \str{a}'s. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each language give a transition graph and table
for a Finite State machine recognizing the language.
Use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a}, \str{b}}$. 
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least two \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has exactly two \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has two or fewer \str{a}'s}}$
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma$ has at least one \str{a} followed by at least one \str{b}}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
At the start of these exercises is the advice to think through the 
language description by listing five members and five nonmembers of 
that language.
Although the question doesn't ask for it, we give that for each.
Also, in the section body is the advice to design machines by 
describing an intuitive meaning for each state.
Although that is not a requirement and the question does not ask for
it, for each item here we give those. 
\begin{exparts}
\item
  Five strings that are elements of the language are 
  \str{aa}, \str{aaa}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, and~\str{baa}.
  Five that are not are 
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{abbbbbb}, and~\str{ba}.
  This machine accepts only strings in that language.
  \begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa23.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$   &$q_2$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\item Five elements of the language are
  \str{aa}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, \str{baa}, and~\str{aabb}.
  Five that are not in the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{ba}, \str{aaa}, and~\str{baaa}.
  \begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa24.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_3$   &$q_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\item
  Five strings that are elements of the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, and~\str{ab}.
  Five that are not are
  \str{aaa}, \str{aaab}, \str{aaba}, \str{abaa}, and~\str{baaa}.
  \begin{center}
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa25.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
 \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_1$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_3$   &$q_2$  \\
    $q_3$ &$q_3$   &$q_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\item
  Five from the language are
  \str{ab}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, \str{abb}, and~\str{bab}.
  Five that are not in the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{ba}, and~\str{bbbbaaaa}.
  \begin{center}
  \quad\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa26.pdf}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{a}     &\str{b}      \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$   \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$ &$q_2$   \\
 \acceptingstate
    $q_2$ &$q_2$   &$q_2$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a Finite State machine
over $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$ that accepts
any string containing the substring \str{abc}.
Give a brief explication of each state's 
role in the machine,
as in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  To accept strings containing the substring \str{abc} the machine 
  must have a state whose intuitive meaning is ``have just seen~\str{a},
  next looking for \str{bc}.''
  That state is~$q_1$.
  The state $q_2$ means that the machine has just processed a 
  substring~\str{ab} and is looking for~\str{c}.
  And $q_3$ means the machine has seen the substring~\str{abc}.
  Finally, $q_0$ is the state the machine is in if it has so far
  not seen even the first character in a potential substring~\str{abc}.
  Here is the machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa29.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Note that if the machine processes~\str{ab} followed by~\str{b}
  then it returns to~$q_0$, but if it processes~\str{ab} followed by~\str{a}
  then it does not return to~$q_0$, it passes to~$q_1$.
  This follows from the intuitive meaning of~$q_1$.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
For each language, give five strings from that language and five that are
not (if there are not that many then list all of the strings that are possible).
Also give a Finite State machine that recognizes the language.
Use $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
  $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{aa}}}$
\item
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\emptystring$}}$
\item
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\str{a}^3\str{b}$ or $\sigma=\str{b}\str{a}^3$}}$
\item
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma=\str{a}^n$ or $\sigma=\str{b}^n$ for $n\in\N$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item 
  Five that are in the language are 
  \str{aa}, \str{aaa}, \str{baa}, \str{aaaa}, and~\str{abaa}.
  Five that are not are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{ab} \str{ba}, and~\str{aba}.
  Here is the machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa32.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  \item The only string in the language is~\emptystring.
  That is, $\lang=\set{\emptystring}$.
  Five not in the language are \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, \str{ab},
  and~\str{ba}.
  The machine is this.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa33.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  \item There are only two strings in the language:
  $\lang=\set{\str{aaab},\str{baaa}}$.
  Five strings not in the language are
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, and~\str{ab}.
  The machine is this.
  \begin{center}
   \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa34.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  \item Five strings in the language are 
  \emptystring, \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, and~\str{bb}.
  Five not in the language are
  \str{ab}, \str{ba}, \str{aab}, \str{aba}, and~\str{baa}.
  This is the machine.
  \begin{center}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa35.pdf}}
  \end{center}
  Note that $q_0$ is an accepting state, because \emptystring{} is a
  member of the language.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce a Finite State machine over the alphabet
$\Sigma=\set{\str{A},\,\ldots \str{Z},\str{0},\ldots \str{9}}$ 
that accepts only the
string \str{911},
and a machine that accepts any string but that one.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The first machine accepts only \str{911} while the second

  states are complementary.

\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What happens when the input to an extended transition function
is the empty string?
\begin{answer}\verified
  It outputs the start state,
  $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=q_0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider this grammar.   
\begin{grammar}
\ntrm{real} \produces{} \ntrm{posreal} \syntaxor \trm{+} \ntrm{posreal} \syntaxor \trm{-} \ntrm{posreal}

\ntrm{posreal} \produces{} \ntrm{natural} \syntaxor \ntrm{natural} \trm{.} \syntaxor \ntrm{natural} \trm{.} \ntrm{natural}

\ntrm{natural} \produces{} \ntrm{digit} \syntaxor \ntrm{digit} \ntrm{natural}

\ntrm{digit} \produces{} \trm{0} \syntaxor \ldots{}  \trm{9}
\end{grammar}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Give five strings of terminals 
  that are in its language and five that are not.
\item Does the language contain the string \str{.12}?
\item Briefly describe the language.
\item Give a Finite State machine that recognizes the language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Five that are: \str{+1}, \str{123}, \str{-123.45}, \str{-0.0},
  and~\str{0}.
  Five that are not: \str{+}, \str{+-}, \str{..}, \str{12.3.4}, and~\str{2+3}
  \item No, the \ntrm{posreal} rule gives that 
   before the period must come at least one digit.
  \item It is the set of representations of real numbers with integer part,
  a decimal point, and a decimal part.
  \item \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa41.pdf}}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Produce 
a Finite State machine for each.
\begin{exparts}
\item   
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}
    \suchthat \text{every \str{1} in $\sigma$ has a \str{0} just before it and just after}}$
\item   
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents in binary a number divisible by $4$}}$
\item 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents  in decimal an even number}}$
\item 
  $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents in decimal a multiple of $100$}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
  Although the exercise doesn't ask for it, for each language we list
  five members and five nonmembers.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Five that are in the language are \str{010}, \str{0010}, \str{0}, 
    \str{0101000}, and~\emptystring.
    Five that are not are \str{10}, \str{0110}, \str{0101}, \str{01001},
    and~\str{1}.
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa42.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \item 
    A string is a binary representation of a multiple of~$4$ if and only
    if it ends in two zero's, \str{00}, or it is~\str{0}.
    Five in the language are \str{100}, \str{0100}, \str{1000}, \str{10100},
    and~\str{0}.
    Five not in the language are \str{10}, \str{1}, \str{1001}, \str{001},
    and~\emptystring. 
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa43.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \item Five strings in the language are \str{0}, \str{20}, \str{12}, \str{88},
    and~\str{08}.
    Five that are not are \str{1}, \str{01}, \str{2221}, \str{17}, 
    and~\emptystring.
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa44.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \item This is basically the same as the second item.
    A string is a decimal representation of a multiple of~$100$ if and only
    if it ends in two zero's, \str{00}, or it is~\str{0}.
    Five in the language are \str{100}, \str{0100}, \str{2000}, \str{60300},
    and~\str{0}.
    Five not in the language are \str{10}, \str{8}, \str{1009}, \str{001},
    and~\emptystring. 
    \begin{center}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa45.pdf}}
    \end{center}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$
   \set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\,\ldots \str{9}}}
     \suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents in decimal a multiple of $4$}}
$.
Briefly describe a Finite State machine.
You need not give the full graph or table.
\begin{answer}\verified
  A number is divisible by four if and only if its representation ends in
  \str{00}, \str{04}, \ldots \str{96}, or the single-digit cases where the
  representation is \str{0}, \str{4},
  or~\str{8}.
  That's $28$ cases.
  A picture would be cluttered but this is a finite language
  and for any finite language there is a Finite State machines that
  recognizes that language.
  (We shall see an alternative approach that makes a clearer
  picture in the section on nondeterminism.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Following \cref{def:ExtendedTransitionFcn} is a 
  constructive definition of the extended transition function.
  We will apply that  
  to the machine in \cref{ex:FSATwoAsEvenBs}.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item Use the definition to find $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})$
    and $\extended{\Delta}(\str{1})$.
  \item Use the definition to find $\extended{\Delta}$'s output on all
    length two inputs
    \str{00}, \str{01}, \str{10}, and \str{11}.
  \item Find its action on all length~three strings.
  \end{exparts*}
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Recall the transition function for that example.
    \begin{center}
      \begin{transitionfunction}
        &\str{0}    &\str{1}   \\
        \cline{2-3}
      $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_3$   \\
      $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_4$    \\
      \acceptingstate
      $q_2$ &$q_2$ &$q_5$  \\
      $q_3$ &$q_4$ &$q_0$    \\
      $q_4$ &$q_5$ &$q_1$    \\
      $q_5$ &$q_5$ &$q_2$    \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
    \end{center}
    \begin{exparts}
    \item If the input string is $\sigma=\str{0}$ then peel off the final
      character to get
      $\sigma=\tau\concat\str{0}=\emptystring\concat\str{0}$ 
      and the definition gives this.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{0})
        =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring),\str{0})
        =\Delta(q_0,\str{0})
        =q_1
      \end{equation*}
      (In the leftmost expression \str{0} is a length-one string,
      while in the rest of the equation \str{0} is a character.
      We ignore this uninteresting difference.)
      Similarly, $\extended{\Delta}(\str{1})=q_3$.
    \item For $\sigma=\str{00}$, peel off the final
      character to get
      $\sigma=\tau\concat\str{0}=\str{0}\concat\str{0}$
      (here the first \str{0} is a length~one string while the second
      is a character). 
      Then use the prior item's computation of
      $\extended{\Delta}(\str{0})$.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{00})
        =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\str{0}),\str{0})
        =\Delta(q_1,\str{0})
        =q_2
      \end{equation*}
      The other three are similar.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{01})=q_4
        \quad\extended{\Delta}(\str{10})=q_4
        \quad\extended{\Delta}(\str{11})=q_0
      \end{equation*}
    \item
      For each of these use the prior item's computation.
      Here is the first.
      \begin{equation*}
        \extended{\Delta}(\str{000})
        =\Delta(\extended{\Delta}(\str{00}),\str{0})
        =\Delta(q_2,\str{0})
        =q_2
      \end{equation*}
      The others are computed in the same way.
      \begin{center}
      \begin{tabular}{*{8}{c}}
        $\extended{\Delta}(\str{000})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{001})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{010})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{011})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{100})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{101})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{110})$
        &$\extended{\Delta}(\str{111})$  \\ \hline
        $q_2$
        &$q_5$
        &$q_5$
        &$q_1$
        &$q_5$
        &$q_1$
        &$q_1$
        &$q_3$
      \end{tabular}
      \end{center}
    \end{exparts}
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  From \textit{Introduction to Languages} by Martin, edition four, p~77.
\end{credit}
Produce a Finite State machine that recognizes the language over
$\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$
containing no more than one occurrence of the substring~\str{aa}.
That is, it may contain zero-many such substrings or one, but not two.
Note that the string \str{aaa} contains two occurrences of that
substring.
\begin{answer}\verified
   \quad\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa48.pdf}}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let $\Sigma=\B$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
List all of the different
Finite State machines over $\Sigma$ with a single state, $Q=\set{q_0}$.
(Ignore whether a state is final or not; we will do that below.)
\item
List all the the ones with two states, $Q=\set{q_0,q_1}$.
\item
How many machines are there with~$n$ states?
\item
What if we distinguish between machines with different sets of final states?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  There is one such machine.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \item
  This list has $16$ machines.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_0]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_1]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$    &$q_1$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_2]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_0$    &$q_0$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$    &$q_0$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}\raisebox{-1ex}{\ldots}\hspace{1em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_{15}]
          &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$ &$q_1$    &$q_1$    \\
    $q_1$ &$q_1$    &$q_1$    \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \item
  Fix $n$ states, $Q=\set{q_0,\ldots q_{n-1}}$.
  Each line of the transition table has two columns so the number of
  distinct lines is $n\cdot n=n^2$.
  An overall table chooses $n$~such lines.
  There are $n^2\cdot n^2 \cdots n^2=n^{2n}$ many ways to do that.
  \item
  For each of the $n$~states, either it is final or it is not.
  The additional factor of $2^n$ makes the total number of distinct tables
  equal to~$2^nn^{2n}\!$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\textit{Propositiones ad acuendos iuvenes} (problems to sharpen
the young) is one of the oldest collection
of mathematical problems.
It is by 
\citetext{Alcuin of York (735--804)}{%
  See \url{https://www.bbc.co.uk/programmes/m000dqy8}.}, royal advisor
to Charlemagne
and head of the Frankish court school, at Aachen.
One problem,
\textit{Propositio de lupo et capra etfasciculo cauli}, is particularly 
famous:~\textit{A man had to transport to the far side of a river
\citetext{a wolf, a goat, and a bundle of cabbages}{%  Annie Raymond
  This translation is from A~Raymond, from the University of Washington.}.
The only boat he could find was one that could
carry only two of them. For that reason, he sought a plan which would
enable them all to get to the far side unhurt. Let him, who is able, say
how it could be possible to transport them safely.}
Readers of the 700's would have known that a wolf cannot be left
alone with a goat, nor can a goat be left alone with cabbages.
Construct the relevant Finite State machine and use it to solve the problem.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The states that the situation can occupy 
  involve having the man, the wolf, the goat, and the cabbage,
  on the near side or far side.
  The allowed transitions are: man crosses with wolf,
  man crosses with goat, man crosses with cabbage, and man crosses alone.
  If the man leaves the wolf alone with the goat then that is an error state
  (on either near or far side), 
  as is leaving the goat alone with the cabbage.
  (Having the man, the wolf, and the goat together is not an error 
  because the man is with them.) 
  \begin{display}\small
    \begin{tabular}{ll|l}
      \multicolumn{2}{c}{\tabulartext{Locations}}
        &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }     \\  \cline{1-2}
      \multicolumn{1}{|c}{\tabulartext{Near}}
        &\multicolumn{1}{c|}{\tabulartext{Far}}
        &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Notes}}  \\  \hline
      Man, Wolf, Goat, Cabbage  &--                   &Start state  \\
      Man, Wolf, Goat,          &Cabbage              &  \\
      Man, Wolf, Cabbage        &Goat                 &  \\
      Man, Goat, Cabbage        &Wolf                 &  \\
      Man, Goat                 &Wolf, Cabbage        &  \\
      Man, Wolf                 &Goat, Cabbage        &Error  \\
      Man, Cabbage              &Wolf, Goat           &Error  \\
      Man                       &Wolf, Goat, Cabbage  &Error  \\
    % \end{tabular}
    % \qquad
    % \begin{tabular}{ll|l}
    %   \multicolumn{2}{c}{\tabulartext{Locations}}
    %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }     \\  \cline{1-2}
    %   \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Near}}
    %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Far}}
    %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Notes}}  \\  \hline
      Wolf, Goat, Cabbage  &Man                       &Error  \\
      Wolf, Goat           &Man, Cabbage              &Error  \\
      Wolf, Cabbage        &Man, Goat                 &  \\
      Wolf                 &Man, Goat, Cabbage        &  \\
      Goat, Cabbage        &Man, Wolf                 &Error  \\
      Goat                 &Man, Wolf, Cabbage        &  \\
      Cabbage              &Man, Wolf, Goat           &  \\
      --                   &Man, Wolf, Goat, Cabbage  &Final state  \\
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}

  The Finite State machine shown in \figureref{fig:WolfGoatCabbage}
  covers all of these possibilities.
  For example, the line labeled `n:~mwg,f:~c' means that the man is on the
  near side with the wolf and goat, while the cabbage is alone on the
  far side. 
  The errors are gathered into a single state.
  So for instance the table in the figure has no line labeled `n:~wg,f:~mc'. 
  The start state is $q_0$ and the only accepting state is~$q_{9}$.

  In addition to the two errors in the first paragraph, it is an error
  to transition with the wolf if the man does not start with the wolf,
  and likewise for the goat and cabbage.
  % \begin{figure}
  %   \centering
  %   \begin{tabular}{rl|cccc}
  %     \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{state}}
  %     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{meaning}}
  %                             &\str{with wolf}
  %                             &\str{with goat}
  %                             &\str{with cabbage}
  %                             &\str{with nothing}  \\  
  %     \hline   
  %      $s_0$  &n:~mwgc, f:~-- &$s_{12}$ &$s_{10}$  &$s_9$    &$s_8$      \\
  %      $s_1$  &n:~mwg, f:~c    &$s_{13}$ &$s_{11}$  &$e$      &$s_9$   \\
  %      $s_2$  &n:~mwc, f:~g    &$s_{14}$ &$e$       &$s_{11}$ &$s_{10}$     \\
  %      $s_3$  &n:~mw, f:~gc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
  %      $s_4$  &n:~mgc, f:~w    &$e$      &$s_{14}$  &$s_{13}$ &$s_{12}$      \\
  %      $s_5$  &n:~mg, f:~wc    &$e$      &$s_{15}$  &$e$      &$s_{13}$      \\
  %      $s_6$  &n:~mc, f:~wg    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$        \\
  %      $s_7$  &n:~m, f:~wgc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$       \\
  %      $s_8$  &n:~wgc, f:~m--  &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
  %      $s_9$  &n:~wg, f:~mc    &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$     \\
  %      $s_{10}$  &n:~wc, f:~mg &$e$      &$s_0$     &$e$     &$s_2$       \\
  %      $s_{11}$  &n:~w, f:~mgc &$e$      &$s_1$     &$s_{10}$ &$s_3$        \\
  %      $s_{12}$  &n:~gc, f:~mw &$e$      &$e$       &$e$     &$e$        \\
  %      $s_{13}$  &n:~g, f:~mwc &$s_1$    &$e$       &$s_4$    &$s_5$         \\
  %      $s_{14}$  &n:~c, f:~mwg &$s_2$    &$s_4$     &$e$      &$s_6$         \\
  %      \acceptingstate
  %      $s_{15}$  &n:~--, f:~mwgc  &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$   &$s_{15}$\\
  %      $e$    &error           &$e$     &$e$       &$e$      &$e$   \\
  %   \end{tabular}
  %   \caption{Finite state machine for Wolf-Goat-Cabbage.}
  %   \label{fig:WolfGoatCabbage}
  % \end{figure}
  \begin{figure}
    \centering
    \begin{tabular}{rl|cccc}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{state}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{near and far}}
                              &\str{with wolf}
                              &\str{with goat}
                              &\str{with cabbage}
                              &\str{with nothing}  \\  
      \hline   
       $q_0$    &n:~mwgc, f:~--  &$e$     &$q_{5}$   &$e$    &$e$      \\
       $q_1$    &n:~mwg, f:~c    &$q_{7}$  &$q_{6}$   &$e$    &$e$   \\
       $q_2$    &n:~mwc, f:~g    &$q_{8}$  &$e$      &$q_{6}$ &$q_{5}$     \\
       $q_3$    &n:~mgc, f:~w    &$e$     &$q_{8}$   &$q_{7}$ &$e$      \\
       $q_4$    &n:~mg, f:~wc    &$e$     &$q_{9}$   &$e$    &$q_{7}$      \\
       $q_{5}$  &n:~wc, f:~mg    &$e$     &$q_0$    &$e$    &$q_2$       \\
       $q_{6}$  &n:~w, f:~mgc    &$e$     &$q_1$    &$q_{2}$ &$e$        \\
       $q_{7}$  &n:~g, f:~mwc    &$q_1$   &$e$      &$q_3$  &$q_4$         \\
       $q_{8}$  &n:~c, f:~mwg    &$q_2$   &$q_3$    &$e$    &$e$         \\
       \acceptingstate
       $q_{9}$  &n:~--, f:~mwgc  &$q_{9}$ &$q_{9}$   &$q_{9}$   &$q_{9}$\\
       $e$    &error            &$e$     &$e$       &$e$      &$e$   \\
    \end{tabular}  \\[1ex]
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa58.pdf}
    \caption{Finite state machine for the Wolf-Goat-Cabbage problem.}
    \label{fig:WolfGoatCabbage}
  \end{figure}

  The circle and arrow diagram does not show the error arrows, as it would
  clog up the picture.
  With that diagram, finding a solution is easy.
  One path through the graph is $q_0$-$q_5$-$q_2$-$q_8$-$q_3$-$q_7$-$q_4$-$q_9$.
  In words that path is:~the man first brings the goat to the far side 
  and leaves it there.
  He goes back to the near side alone.
  He then brings the wolf with him from the near to the far side.
  Next he brings back the goat to the near side.
  He leaves the goat on the near side and takes the cabbage and brings it
  to the far side.
  Finally, he goes back alone to the near side, 
  and finishes by taking the goat to the far side.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

%   of $\gamma$.

%   \begin{center}
