\begin{exercise}
  Fix an alphabet with at least two members.
  We will show that there are languages over that alphabet not recognized by any 
  Finite State machine.
  \begin{exparts*}
    \item Show that the number of Finite State machines with that 
       alphabet is infinite.
    \item Show that it is countable.
    \item Show that the number of languages over that alphabet is
      uncountable.
  \end{exparts*} 
\begin{answer}\verified
  For the alphabet with at least two members we write~$\Sigma$.
  We fix one character from that alphabet,~\str{s}.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item
  We will show that over that alphabet there are infinitely many
  different Finite State machines.
  The machines below differ both because they have a different
  number of states,
  and because they recognize different languages.
  \begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &\tabulartext{other} \\ \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}  
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &\tabulartext{other} \\ \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$ \\
    $q_1$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}  
  \begin{transitionfunction}
    &\str{s}  &\tabulartext{other}  \\ \cline{2-3}
    \acceptingstate
    $q_0$ &$q_1$ &$q_0$  \\
    $q_1$ &$q_2$ &$q_0$  \\
    $q_2$ &$q_0$ &$q_0$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \hspace{1em}\ldots  
  \end{center}
  % That proves, under a reasonable interpretation of what it means for
  % machines to differ, that there are at least countably many Finite State
  % machines.
  % (The technical wording is that these machines are not isomorphic.)
  \item 
  % The argument that there are no more than countably many machines also
  % requires an interpretation.
  By definition the set of possible states is
  $Q=\set{q_0,q_1,\ldots}$. 
  % (so as to not get uncountably many
  % machines by virtue of there being uncountably many possible states.
  Then we can count the
  Finite State machines because for any collection of finitely
  many states $\set{q_0,\ldots\, q_k}$ there are finitely many transition tables.
  Thus the number of Finite State machines using those states is countable.
  Then the union of countably many countable sets is countable,
  and so the number of machines over all collections of finitely many states
  is countable.
  \item Because $\Sigma$ has at least two members, the usual 
   diagonalization construction shows that the power set of $\Sigma$
   is not countable. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}
\index{Finite State machine|)}







% ==================================================
\section{Nondeterminism}\index{nondeterminism|(}
Turing machines and Finite State machines both have the property that,
given the current state and current character,
the next state is completely determined.
Once we lay out an initial tape and push \texttt{Start} then
the machine just walks through a fixed succession of step/next step calculations.
We now consider machines that are nondeterministic, ones for 
which there may be configurations where there is more than
one next state, or configurations where there is just one, 
or 
even configurations without any next state at all.


\subsection{Motivation}
Imagine a grammar
with some rules and a start symbol.
We get a string and are asked to find a derivation of it.
The challenge is that
we sometimes don't know which rules the derivation should follow.
For instance, if we have
\mbox{S\produces{}B\trm{a}S\syntaxor A\trm{b}A} then
from S we can do two different things:~which will work? 

In the Grammar section's exercises we expected that an 
intelligent person would
have the insight to guess the right way.  
If instead we were writing a program then we might 
have it try every case\Dash we might do a breadth-first traversal of the 
directed acyclic ggraph of all derivations\Dash until 
the program finds a success.

\margingraphic[l]{0.175\textwidth}{automata/pix/yogi.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yogi_Berra}{Yogi Berra 1925--2015}}\index{Berra, Y!picture}
The American philosopher and Hall of Fame baseball catcher Y~Berra
said, ``When you come to a fork in the road, take it.''
That's a natural way to attack this problem:~when you come up against
multiple possibilities,
fork a child for each.
Thus, the routine might begin with the start state~$S$ and for each rule that
could apply, it spawns a child process, deriving a
string one removed from the start.
After that, each child finds each rule that could apply to its string and
spawns its own children, each of which now has a string that is two removed from
the start.
Continue until the desired string appears, if it ever does.

The prototypical example for this strategy is
the celebrated \definend{\probname{Traveling Salesman}} 
problem,\index{Traveling Salesman problem@\probname{Traveling Salesman} problem} 
\citetext{that of finding the shortest circuit visiting every city in a list}{%
  See 
  \url{https://nbviewer.jupyter.org/url/norvig.com/ipython/TSP.ipynb}.}.
For instance, suppose that we want to know if there is a trip that visits each
state capital in the 
\citetext{US lower forty eight states}{%
   See \url{https://wiki.openstreetmap.org/wiki/TIGER}.}
and returns back to where it began, in 
less than $16\,000$ kilometers.
We start at Montpelier, the capital of Vermont.
From there we could fork a process for each potential next capital,
making forty seven new processes.
Thus the process that after Montpelier goes next to Concord, New Hampshire 
would know that the trip so far is $188$~kilometers. 
In the next round, each child would fork its own child processes, 
forty six of them.
% For instance the process that after Montpelier was assigned Concord
% would have a child assigned to Augusta, Maine and would know that
% so far the trip is $452$~kilometers.
At the end, many processes will have failed
to find a short-enough trip but if even one 
finds it then we consider
the overall search a success.

% \margingraphic{0.16\textwidth}{automata/pix/angel.jpg}{\label{fig:Angel}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angel}{Persian angel, 1555}} % Kneeling Angel from Bukhara School c. 1555-1560. British Museum https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Persian_angel_1555.jpg [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
That computation 
is nondeterministic in that while it is happening
the machine is 
simultaneously in many different states.
Restated, the computation happens on an unboundedly-parallel machine, 
where whenever
we need an additional computing agent, another CPU plus tape, one is 
available.\footnote{%
  This is like our experience with everyday computers, 
  where we may be writing an email in one window 
  and watching a video in another.
  The machine appears to be
  in multiple states simultaneously. 
  % (Although it may in fact be time-slicing,
  % dovetailing by running each process in succession for a few ticks.)
}
% Conflicts with angelic nondeterminism
% Such a machine is
% angelic in that if we just ask for more resources, then we shall receive.

% This section considers nondeterministic Finite State machines.
% (Nondeterministic Turing machines appear in the fifth chapter.)
We will have two ways to think about nondeterminism,
two mental models.\footnote{%
  While these models are helpful in learning and thinking about nondeterminism,
  they are not part of the formal definitions and proofs.}
The first is the one introduced above:~when a machine 
is presented with multiple possible next states then
it forks, so that it is in all of them simultaneously.
The next example illustrates.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}
The Finite State machine below is nondeterministic because 
leaving $q_0$ are two arrows labeled~\str{0}.
It also has states with a deficit of edges such as that
no arrow for~\str{1} leaves $q_1$ so if it is in that state and reads that
input then it passes to no state at all.
\begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm00.pdf}}
\end{center}
The graphic below shows what happens with input \str{00001}. 
It pictures the computation history as a tree.
For instance, on the first \str{0} the computation splits in two
so the machine is now in two states at once.
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{Steps in the nondeterministic computation.} 
    \includegraphics{\asydir nfsm/nfsm08}
    \label{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree}
  \end{fig}
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{Steps in the nondeterministic computation.} 
    \animategraphics[poster=last]{3}{\asydir nfsm/nfsm}{02}{08}
    \label{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree}
  \end{animatedfigure}
}
% \begin{center}
%   \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm08.pdf}
% \end{center}
\end{example}
When we considered the forking approach to string derivations or to
the Traveling Salesman problem,
we observed that if a solution exists then there is at least one child process 
that finds it.
The same happens here:~there is a
branch of the computation tree that accepts the input string.
There are also branches that are not successful.
The one at the bottom dies after step~$2$
because when the present
state is~$q_2$ and the input is~\str{0} then this machine passes to 
\citetext{no-state}{%
  What is no-state, exactly?
  We can think that it is like what happens if you write 
  a program with a sequence of if-then statements and forget to include an else.
  Obviously the computer goes somewhere, the instruction pointer points to
  some address, but what happens is not sensible in terms of
  the model that you've stated.

  As an alternate, the wonderful book \autocite{Hofstadter} describes a place 
  named Tumbolia, which is where holes go when they are filled,
  and also where your lap goes when you stand up.
  Perhaps the machines go there.}.\footnote{%
  No-state cannot be an accepting state, since it isn't a state at all.
}
The branch at the top does not die early but it
also does not accept the input.
However, we don't care if there are dozens of unsuccessful branches,
we only care that there is at least one success.
Consequently, we will define that a nondeterministic machine accepts an input
if the computation tree has at least one branch that accepts.

The machine in the above example
accepts any string that ends in two~\str{0}'s and a~\str{1}.
As it is consuming the input $\sigma=\str{00001}$, 
the problem that the machine faces 
is:~when should it stop going around $q_0$'s loop and start to the right?
This machine accepts this input,
so it has solved this problem\Dash viewed from the outside at least,
we could say that the
machine has correctly guessed what to do.

This is our second model for nondeterminism.
We can imagine programming by calling a function, some 
\citetext{\codeinline{amb(...)}}{%
  This operator takes a list of possibilities and evaluates to an option,
  if one is available, that allows the program as a whole to succeed.
  Here is a small example (from \url{https://rosettacode.org/wiki/Amb}):~first 
  \codeinline{let} the values
  \str{(x (amb 1 2 3))} and 
  \str{(y (amb 5 4 3))}. 
  Then call \str{(amb (= (* x y) 8))}.
  The result is that \str{x} has the value~$2$,
  while $y$ is~$4$.
  % \codeinline{(x (amb 1 2 3))} and \codeinline{(y (amb 5 4 3))}.
  % Essentially \codeinline{amb(x,y,z)} splits the computation
  % into three possible futures:
  % a future in which the value \str{x} is yielded,
  % a future in which the value \str{y} is yielded,
  % and a future in which the value \str{z} is yielded.
  % The future which leads to a successful subsequent computation is chosen.
  % The other ``parallel universes'' somehow go away.
  % (\codeinline{amb} called with no arguments fails.)
  % See \url{https://rosettacode.org/wiki/Amb}.
% \protect\begin{lstlisting}
% (let ((x (amb 1 2 3))
%       (y (amb 5 4 3)))
%   (amb (= (* x y) 8)
%   (println x y)))
% \protect\end{lstlisting}
  That is, \str{amb(1,2,3)} chooses the future
  in which \str{x} has value $2$, and \str{amb(7,6,4,5)} chooses $4$,
  in order to ensure that
  \str{amb(x*y = 8)} produces a success.
  
  These operators were described by John McCarthy in \autocite{McCarthy}.
  ``Ambiguous functions are not really functions.
  For each prescription of values
  to the arguments the ambiguous function has a collection of possible values.
  An example of an ambiguous function is
  $\operatorname{less}(n)$ defined for all positive integer
  values of $n$.
  Every non-negative integer less than $n$ is a possible value of
  $\operatorname{less}(n)$.
  First we define a basic ambiguity operator $\operatorname{amb}(x, y)$
  whose possible
  values are $x$ and~$y$ when both are defined: otherwise, whichever is defined.
  Now we can define $\operatorname{less}(n)$ by
  $\operatorname{less}(n) = \operatorname{amb}(n-1, \operatorname{less}(n-1))$.''
  },
that guesses a successful sequence if there is one to 
guess.\footnote{%
  The name \protect\str{amb}\protect\index{amb@amb, ambiguous function}
  abbreviates `ambiguous function'.}\index{guessing!by a machine}

% \margingraphic{0.25\textwidth}{automata/demon.jpg}{\label{fig:Demon}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nightmare}{\textit{The Nightmare} H~Fuseli, 1781}} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/56/John_Henry_Fuseli_-_The_Nightmare.JPG  Henry Fuseli [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons  (https://www.atlasobscura.com/articles/original-nightmare-demon-suffocation-night-terror)
Saying that a mechanism guesses is jarring.
Based on programming classes,
a person's intuition may well be that ``guessing'' is
not mechanically accomplishable.
%  since  
% \citetext{it does not fit our intuition of how computers work}{%
%   \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Aq_1l316ow8}{``That's not how this works, that's not how any of this works''}}.
% But researchers in this area often frame it in this way.
As an alternative, we can imagine that the machine is
furnished with the answer (``go around twice, then off to the right'') and
only has to check it.

\margingraphic{0.22\textwidth}{automata/pix/flauros.jpg}{\label{fig:Flauros}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_demons_in_the_Ars_Goetia}{Flauros, Duke of Hell}}\index{Flauros, Duke of Hell!picture} %, gives true answers of all things past, present, and future}} % https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AIll_dict_infernal_p0294-278_flauros_demon.jpg Louis Le Breton [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
When we talk about this way of expressing the second mental model   
our convention is to call the furnisher a 
\citetext{demon}{%
  The term `demon' arose from Maxwell's demon.
  This is a thought experiment created in 1867 by the physicist J~C Maxwell
  about the second law of thermodynamics, which says that it takes energy
  to raise the temperature of a sealed system.
  Maxwell imagined a chamber of gas with a door controlled
  by an all-knowing demon.
  When the demon sees a gas molecule of gas approaching that is slow-moving, 
  it opens the door and lets that molecule out of the chamber, 
  thereby raising the chamber's temperature
  without any external heat.
  See \autocite{wiki:MaxwellsDemon}.}\index{demon, or daemon}\index{daemon|see{demon}},
because they somehow know answers that cannot otherwise be found
but also because we must be suspicious and check that the 
answer is not a trick.
Under this model, a nondeterministic computation
accepts the input if there exists a branch of the computation tree
that a deterministic machine, if told what branch to take, could verify.

Below we shall describe nondeterminism using both paradigms:~as a
machine being in multiple states at once, and also as a machine guessing
(or being told and verifying).
In this chapter we will do that for Finite State
machines and in the fifth chapter we will return to it for Turing machines. 




\subsection{Definition}

A nondeterministic Finite State machine's
next-state function does not output single states,
it outputs sets of states, members of the power set~$\powerset (Q)$.

%<*def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine>
\begin{definition} \label{def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine}
A 
\definend{nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{NFSM|see {nondeterministic Finite State machine}}\index{Finite State machine!nondeterministic}\index{nondeterminism!for Finite State machines}
$\FSM=\sequence{Q,q_{0},F,\Sigma,\Delta}$ 
consists of a finite \definend{set of states}~$Q$,
one of which is the \definend{start state}~$q_{0}$,
a subset $F\subseteq Q$ of \definend{accepting states}\index{accepting state!nondeterministic Finite State machine}
or \definend{final states},\index{final state!nondeterministic Finite State machine}
a finite \definend{input alphabet} set~$\Sigma$,
and a \definend{next-state function}\index{next-state function!nondeterministic Finite State machine}
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{\powerset (Q)}$.
\end{definition}
%</def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine>

We will use these machines in three ways.
First, they are useful in practice;~many tasks 
are more easily solved with nondeterministic
machines than with deterministic ones.
We will use them to prove Kleene's Theorem,
\cref{th:KleenesTheorem}.
And, they give us an initial encounter with
nondeterminism, which is a critical concept
for this book's fifth chapter. 

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NDFMFromDiscussion}
This is \cref{ex:NFSMInDiscussion}'s nondeterministic Finite State machine,
along with its transition function.
That function does not output states, it outputs sets of states.
\begin{center}\small
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm00.pdf}}
  \hspace*{3em}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
      &\str{0}    &\str{1}             \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $q_1$  &$\set{q_2}$      &$\set{}$     \\
    $q_2$  &$\set{}$         &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    \acceptingstate{} $q_3$  &$\set{}$     &$\set{}$   \\    
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
\end{example}

The imagery in informal terms such as ``guess'' and ``demon''
helps introduce the ideas
but may also give an impression that those ideas are fuzzy.
So when we next step through the description of the action of these
machines, note that it is precise. 

\begin{remark}
When we described the action of deterministic Finite State machines 
on page~\pageref{page:DefnYieldsFiniteStateMachine}, we laid out how to 
construct the sequence of configurations, by transitioning from each to the
succeeding one until the tape is empty.
But for nondeterministic machines there needn't be one and only one sequence.
That makes a description that is non-constructive the clearer choice.
\end{remark}

\label{page:DefnYieldsNondeterministicFSM}
A 
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!configuration} 
is a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}$ where
$q\in Q$ and $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
A machine starts in an
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!initial configuration}
$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0}$, so that
$\tau_0$ is
the \definend{input}.\index{input string}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!input string} 

Two configurations may be related by a \definend{transition}, denoted
$\,\yields$.\index{yields@$\yields$, yields!for nondeterministic Finite State machines}
In the configuration $\mathcal{C}_s=\sequence{q,\tau_s}$,
let the string $\tau_s$ be not empty.
Then where
$\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\sequence{\hat{q},\hat{\tau}}$,
we write 
$\mathcal{C}_s\yields\hat{\mathcal{C}}$\index{yields@$\yields$, yields!for nondeterministic Finite State machines}
or say that $\hat{\mathcal{C}}$ succeeds $\mathcal{C}_s$
if two conditions hold.
First, it must be that
$\hat{\tau}$ comes from removing $\tau_s$'s leading 
character,
so that $\tau_s=c\concat\hat{\tau}$ for $c\in\Sigma$.
Second, using that leading character~$c$,
it must also be that the next state~$\hat{q}$ is a member of the 
set~$\Delta(q,c)$.

So far the definitions apply to any Finite State machine, deterministic or
not.
But for the machines in this section  
there may be many such branches, or one, or none at all.

A configuration with an tape empty string,
$\mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q_h,\emptystring}$,
is a
\definend{halting configuration}.\index{halting configuration}\index{configuration!halting}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!halting configuration} 
Consider the sequences of configurations related by transitions 
and starting with $\mathcal{C}_0$.
A \definend{branch}\index{branch!of the computation tree}\index{computation tree!branch}
is a sequence that is maximally long.
(In this machine type 
a halting configuration has no configuration succeeding it, 
so if a branch has one then it comes last.
But as in \cref{ex:NFSMInDiscussion},
branches can also terminate without ending in a halting configuration.)

The 
\definend{computation tree}\index{computation tree!nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!computation tree}
or just
\definend{computation}\index{computation!nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!computation}
for a nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$ 
is the set containing all of the branches.
If in that set there exists a branch ending in a halting configuration,
$
  \mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0} 
   \yields \mathcal{C}_1
   \yields \cdots\, \mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q_h,\emptystring}
$
whose state is accepting, $q_h\in F\!$,
then $\FSM$ \definend{accepts}\index{accept an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
the input~$\tau_0$.
Otherwise, it 
\definend{rejects}~$\tau_0$.\index{reject an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!reject string}

\begin{example}
\Cref{ex:NFSMInDiscussion} shows
a number branches.
Since this branch
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{00001}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{0001}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{001}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{01}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{1}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\emptystring}    
\end{equation*}
ends in a halting configuration whose state is accepting,
the machine accepts \str{00001}.
\end{example}

As with deterministic Finite State machines, at each step 
the machine consumes one character.
There is no \HP{} for these machines. 

%<*def:NFSMAccepted}
\begin{definition}\label{def:NFSMAccepted}
For a nondeterministic Finite State machine~$\FSM$, the set of accepted strings
is the 
\definend{language of the machine~$\lang(\FSM)$},\index{language!of a nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!language of} 
or the language  
\definend{recognized}\index{recognized language!of a nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!language recognized}
% or 
% \definend{decided}\index{decided!by a nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!language decided}
by that machine. %\footnotemark[2]
\end{definition}
% \footnotetext[2]{Below we will define something called
%   $\varepsilon$ transitions that make `recognized' the right idea here,
%   rather than `decided'.}
%</def:NFSMAccepted>

\label{discussion:NFSMStringAcceptance}
We can also adapt the definition 
of the 
extended transition function\index{extended transition function!nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
so that it outputs sets,
$\map{\extended{\Delta}}{\kleenestar{\Sigma}}{\powerset(Q)}$.
Fix a nondeterministic~$\FSM$ with
transition function $\map{\Delta}{Q\times \Sigma}{Q}$.
Start with $\extended{\Delta}(\emptystring)=\set{q_0}$.
For $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$, where
$\extended{\Delta}(\tau)=\set{q_{i_0},q_{i_1},\ldots{} q_{i_k}}$
and $t\in\Sigma$, define
$\extended{\Delta}(\tau\concat t)$ to be
$\Delta(q_{i_0},t)\cup\Delta(q_{i_1},t)\cup\cdots \cup\Delta(q_{i_k},t)$. 
Then the machine accepts
$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ if and only if any element of
$\extended{\Delta}(\sigma)$ is a final state.

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}
The language recognized by this nondeterministic machine
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm09.pdf}
\end{center}
is the set of strings containing the substring \str{aa} or \str{bb}.
For instance, the machine accepts \str{abaaba} 
% where the 
% matching substring starts at index~$2$, 
because there is a
sequence of transitions ending in an accepting state.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{abaaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{baaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{aaba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{aba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\str{ba}}
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\str{a}}
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}
With $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$,
this nondeterministic machine
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm10.pdf}
\end{center}
recognizes the language $\set{(\str{ac})^n\suchthat n\in\N}
  =\set{\emptystring,\str{ac},\str{acac},\,\ldots\,}$.
The symbol~\str{b} isn't attached to any arrow
so it won't play a part in any accepting string.
\end{example}

Often a nondeterministic Finite State machines is
easier to write than a deterministic machine that 
does the same job.

\begin{example}
Both of these machines accept any string
whose next to last character is~\str{a}.
The nondeterministic one on the left is simpler than the deterministic 
one.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm62.pdf}}
  \qquad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm88.pdf}}
\end{center}
\end{example}
% The deterministic one gave me more trouble than is reasonable.
% To be sure, I converted the nondterministic one.
%                 a   b
%               +-------
% {}=s0         | s0  s0
% {q0}=s1       | s3  s1
% {q1}=s2       | s4  s4
% {q1,q0}=s3    | s7  s5
% {q2}=s4       | s0  s0
% {q2,q0}=s5    | s3  s1
% {q2,q1}=s6    | s4  s4
% {q2,q1,q0}=s7 | s7  s5
% I made up asy/nfsm/next-to-last-a.dot and drew the picture,
% and then converted asy/nfsm/nfsm88 to look like it.

\begin{example}
This machine accepts
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
  \text{$\sigma=0\concat\tau\concat 1$ where $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm63.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:ListenerMachine}
This is a
remote control listener that waits to hear
the signal \str{0101110}.
That is, it recognizes the language
$\set{\sigma\concat\str{0101110}\suchthat \sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}}$.
% of strings with that suffix.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm11.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}




% The \texorpdfstring avoids a hyperref error from math in the bookmarks
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{$\varepsilon$}{Epsilon} transitions}\index{epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions|(}
%<*discussion:epsilonTransitions>
Another extension, beyond nondeterminism, is to allow 
\definend{$\varepsilon$~transitions}\index{epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions} 
or
\definend{$\varepsilon$ moves}.\index{epsilon moves@$\varepsilon$ moves}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!epsilon moves@$\varepsilon$ moves}
We alter 
the definition of a nondeterministic Finite State machine
% \cref{def:NondeterministicFiniteStateMachine}, 
so that instead of $\map{\Delta}{Q\times\Sigma}{\powerset(Q)}$
the transition function's signature is
$\map{\Delta}{Q\times(\Sigma\union\set{\emptystring})}{\powerset(Q)}$.\footnote{%
  For this purpose 
  the `$\varepsilon$' is a character, not a representation of the
  empty string.
  Assume that it is not an element of $\Sigma$.}
The associated behavior
is that the machine can transition spontaneously, 
without consuming any input.
We start with a number of examples.
%</discussion:epsilonTransitions>

\begin{example}
This machine recognizes valid integer representations.
The $\varepsilon$ on the first arrow
means that the machine can jump from~$q_0$ 
to~$q_1$ without reading the tape.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm12.pdf}
\end{center}
For instance, with input~\str{123} the machine 
can begin as below by following the $\varepsilon$
transition to state~$q_1$ without reading and deleting the leading~\str{1}.
It next reads that \str{1} and transitions
to $q_2$, and then stays there while processing the \str{2} and \str{3}.
This branch of the machine's computation tree accepting its input
and so \str{123} is in the machine's language.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{123}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{123}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{23}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{3}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
The practical effect of the $\varepsilon$
is that this machine
can accept strings that do not start with a \str{+} or \str{-}~sign.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:TwoOrMoreEpsilonTrans}
This machine has a number of $\varepsilon$ transitions.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{\asydir nfsm/nfsm83.pdf}
\end{center}
Here it accepts~\str{abc}
by following the $\varepsilon$ transition between $q_2$ and~$q_3$.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{abc}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{bc}}
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{c}}
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\str{c}}
  \yields \sequence{q_4,\emptystring}    
\end{equation*}
A machine may also, in a single step,
follow two or more $\varepsilon$ transitions in succession.
Here, it accepts~\str{d}
by transitioning from~$q_0$
to~$q_5$ without consuming any input.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{d}}
  \yields \sequence{q_5,\str{d}}
  \yields \sequence{q_6,\emptystring}    
\end{equation*}
The language is
$\lang=\set{\str{abc},\str{abd},\str{c}, \str{d}}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:ComputationTreeInclEpsilons}
Below is a machine that is nondeterministic and with 
$\varepsilon$~moves, along with 
its computation tree on input \str{aab}.
The $\varepsilon$ moves are inside the white stripes.
\ifbool{MakingPaperCopy}{%
  \begin{fig}{Computation tree for a nondeterministic machine with $\varepsilon$ moves.} 
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1044.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1049.pdf}}
    \label{anim:NondeterministicWithEpsilonComputationTree}
  \end{fig}
}{%
  \begin{animatedfigure}{Computation tree for a nondeterministic machine with $\varepsilon$ moves.} 
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1044.pdf}}
    \qquad
    \vcenteredhbox{\animategraphics[poster=last]{3}{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm}{1045}{1049}}
    \label{anim:NondeterministicWithEpsilonComputationTree}
  \end{animatedfigure}
}
\noindent At each step, the machine is in all of the states that 
are inside of the step's stripe.
For instance, at step~$0$ the machine is in both $q_0$ and~$q_1$.
After exhausting the tape, at step~$3$ it is in both $q_0$ and~$q_2$
and because $q_0$ is an accepting state, it accepts the input \str{aab}. 
\end{example}

The $\varepsilon$~transitions simplify building Finite State machines.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMJoinParallel}
An $\varepsilon$ transition can put two machines together with a
parallel connection.
Here is a machine whose states are named with $q$'s combined in parallel with
one whose states are named with~$r$'s.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm14.pdf}
\end{center}
The top machine's language is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{ab}}}$
and the bottom's is
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{c}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma=(\str{ac})^n$ for some $n\in\N$}}$.
This is the language for the entire machine.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat
   \text{either $\sigma$ ends in \str{ab} or $\sigma=(\str{ac})^n$ for $n\in\N$}}
\end{equation*}
We can take the alphabet for the entire machine to be the union,
$\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NFSM:JoinSerial}
An $\varepsilon$ transition can also connect machines serially.
The machine on the left below recognizes 
$\lang_0=\set{(\str{aab})^i\suchthat i\in\N}$.
The one on the right recognizes
$\lang_1=\set{\sigma_0\concat\cdots\,\sigma_{j-1}\suchthat \text{$j\in\N$ and $\sigma_k=\str{a}$ or $\sigma_k=\str{aba}$ for $0\leq k\leq j-1$}}$.
\begin{center} % align graphics on top https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/126919/121234
  % Get width of first graphic
  \newsavebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}
  \savebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm89.pdf}}
  \newlength{\jhtmplengthautomatai}
  \settowidth{\jhtmplengthautomatai}{\usebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}}
  % Get width of second graphic
  \newsavebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}
  \savebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm90.pdf}}
  \newlength{\jhtmplengthautomataii}
  \settowidth{\jhtmplengthautomataii}{\usebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}}
  % Tabulars align paragraphs on top
  \begin{tabular}{p{\jhtmplengthautomatai} @{\hspace*{3em}} p{\jhtmplengthautomatai}}
    \vspace{0pt} \usebox{\jhtmpboxautomatai}
    &\vspace{0pt} \usebox{\jhtmpboxautomataii}
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
If we insert an $\varepsilon$~bridge 
to the right side's initial state 
from
each of the left side's final states 
(here there is only one such state), 
and de-finalize those states on the left,
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm13.pdf}
\end{center}
then the 
combined machine accepts strings in the concatenation of those languages,
$\mathcal{L}(\FSM)=\lang_0\concat\lang_1$.
For example, it accepts \str{aabaababa}, and
\str{aabaa}, as well as \str{abaa}.
\end{example}

%<*ex:EpsilonGivesKleeneStar>
\begin{example} \label{ex:EpsilonTransMakeKleeneStar}
We can also use $\varepsilon$ transitions to get the Kleene star of
a language.
Without the $\varepsilon$~edge this machine's language is
$\lang=\set{\emptystring, \str{ab}}$,
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{\asydir nfsm/nfsm15.pdf}
\end{center}
but with that edge the language is  
$\kleenestar{\lang}=\set{(\str{ab})^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.
\end{example}
%</ex:EpsilonGivesKleeneStar>

We next describe the action of these machines. 
For that we need a function.
%<*df:EpsilonClosurei>
The \definend{$\varepsilon$~closure} 
$\map{\hat{E}}{Q}{\powerset (Q)}$\index{epsilon closure@$\varepsilon$ closure} 
inputs a state~$q$ and returns
the set of states that are reachable from~$q$ in some number of $\varepsilon$ moves.
%</df:EpsilonClosurei>

%<*disc:EpsilonClosurei>
We will build it by iteratively,
by constructing a function
$\map{E}{Q\times\N}{\powerset (Q)}$ such that $E(q,m)$ is the set of 
states reachable from $q$ in at most $m$-many $\varepsilon$ moves.
To define this function, for $m=0$ take $E(q,m)=\set{q}$. 
For $m>0$, where  
$E(q,m)=\set{q_{m_0},\ldots\, q_{m_k}}$,
take $E(q,m+1)=E(q,m)\cup \Delta(q_{m_0},\varepsilon)\cup
                         \cdots\,\Delta(q_{m_k},\varepsilon)$.
The resulting sets are nested,
$E(q,0)=\set{q}\subseteq E(q,1)\subseteq \cdots\;$.
There are only finitely many states so 
for any initial $q$ there must be an $m_q\in\N$
where the sequence of sets stops growing,
$E(q,m_q\,)= E(q,m_q+1)= \cdots\,$.
Define the $\varepsilon$~closure as the limit,
$\hat{E}(q)=E(q,m_q)$.\index{epsilon closure@$\varepsilon$ closure}
% We will find it convenient to extend
% this definition of the epsilon closure of a state to
% the epsilon closure of a set of states.
% Where $S=\set{q_{i_0},\ldots\,q_{i_j}}$,
% let $\hat{E}(S)=\hat{E}(q_{i_0})\cup\hat{E}(q_{i_1})\cup\ldots\,\hat{E}(q_{i_j})$. 
%</disc:EpsilonClosurei>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FindEpsilonClosureInSteps}
%<*df:EpsilonClosureii>
Recall the machine from \cref{ex:TwoOrMoreEpsilonTrans}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{\asydir nfsm/nfsm83.pdf}
\end{center}
This table finds the epsilon closure for each state.
\begin{center} \small
  \begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}|c}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{$E(q,m)$}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$m=0$}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$1$}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$2$}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$3$}  
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\hat{E}(q)$}  \\
     \cline{2-6}
     $q_0$ &$\set{q_0}$ &$\set{q_0,q_2}$ &$\set{q_0,q_2,q_3,q_5}$ 
           &$\set{q_0,q_2,q_3,q_5}$ &$\set{q_0,q_2,q_3,q_5}$ \\
     $q_1$ &$\set{q_1}$ &$\set{q_1}$
           &$\set{q_1}$  &$\set{q_1}$ &$\set{q_1}$ \\
     $q_2$ &$\set{q_2}$ &$\set{q_2,q_3,q_5}$ &$\set{q_2,q_3,q_5}$ 
           &$\set{q_2,q_3,q_5}$ &$\set{q_2,q_3,q_5}$ \\
     $q_3$ &$\set{q_3}$ &$\set{q_3}$ &$\set{q_3}$ 
           &$\set{q_3}$ &$\set{q_3}$ \\
     $q_4$ &$\set{q_4}$ &$\set{q_4}$ &$\set{q_4}$ 
           &$\set{q_4}$ &$\set{q_4}$ \\
     $q_5$ &$\set{q_5}$ &$\set{q_5}$ &$\set{q_5}$ 
           &$\set{q_5}$ &$\set{q_5}$ \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
% For instance for $q_0\!$'s row, after the inital entry $\set{q_0}$
% note that the machine
% has an $\varepsilon$~move from $q_0$ to~$q_2$ 
% so the next column's set gains a~$q_2$.
% As to $E(q_0,2)$, following the two additional 
% $\varepsilon$ transitations adds $q_3$ and~$q_5$.
% Further steps don't add anything 
% so the epsilon closure of $q_0$ is 
% $\hat{E}(q_0)=E(q_0,2)$. 
%</df:EpsilonClosureii>
\end{example}

We can now describe the action.
%<*disc:EpsilonClosureii>
A
\definend{configuration}\index{configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!configuration} 
is a pair $\mathcal{C}=\sequence{q,\tau}\in Q\times\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$. 
A machine starts in an
\definend{initial configuration}\index{initial configuration}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!initial configuration}
$\mathcal{C}_0=\sequence{q_0,\tau_0}$, with
\definend{input}~$\tau_0$.\index{input string}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!input string} 

Fix $\mathcal{C}_s=\sequence{q,\tau_s}$
in order to describe under what circumstances 
$\mathcal{C}_s\yields\hat{\mathcal{C}}$\index{yields@$\yields$, yields!for nondeterministic Finite State machines}
for $\hat{\mathcal{C}}=\sequence{\hat{q},\hat{\tau}}$.
There are two possibilities.
The first is the same as for any nondeterministic machine:~$\tau$ is not 
empty and $\hat{\tau}$ results
from popping $\tau$'s leading character, $\tau=c\concat\hat{\tau}$,
and also $\hat{q}$ is a member of $\Delta(q,c)$.
The second possibility applies only to machines with
$\varepsilon$ moves:~$\hat{\tau}=\tau$, so the machine doesn't consume 
any input,
and also $\hat{q}$ is a member of the $\varepsilon$ closure $\hat{E}(q)$.
% (with $\hat{q}\neq q$; we disallow an $\varepsilon$ transition from
% a state to itself).

Consider the sequences of configurations related by transitions.
A \definend{branch}\index{branch!of the computation tree}\index{computation tree!branch}
is a sequence starting with $\mathcal{C}_0$ that is maximally long.
(In this machine type there can be cycles of $\varepsilon$ moves and 
so branches can be infinite.)

The 
\definend{computation tree}\index{computation tree!nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!computation tree}
or just
\definend{computation}\index{computation!nondeterministic Finite State machine}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!computation}
for a nondeterministic Finite State machine with $\varepsilon$ moves~$\FSM$ 
is the set containing all of the branches.
If in that set there exists a branch containing a
\definend{halting configuration},\index{halting configuration}\index{configuration!halting}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!halting configuration} 
$\mathcal{C}_h=\sequence{q_h,\emptystring}$,
and its state is accepting, $q_h\in F\!$,
then $\FSM$ \definend{accepts}\index{accept an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!accept string}\index{accepts}\index{string accepted!by nondeterministic Finite State machine} 
the input~$\tau_0$.
Otherwise $\FSM$ 
\definend{rejects}~$\tau_0$.\index{reject an input}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!reject string}

We finish by defining the
\definend{extended transition function~$\map{\extended{\Delta}}{Q\times\kleenestar{(\Sigma\union\set{\emptystring})}}{Q}$}\index{extended transition function!for nondeterministic Finite State machines}\index{transition function!extended}  
to suit this new machine type.
Fix $q\in Q$.
Begin with 
$\extended{\Delta}(q,\emptystring)=\hat{E}(q)$.
For the iteration, 
suppose that $\extended{\Delta}(q,\tau)=\set{q_{i_0},q_{i_1},\ldots\, q_{i_k}}$
for $\tau\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$.
Where  $c\in\Sigma$, to define
$\extended{\Delta}(q,\tau\concat c)$ first
make the set of all states reachable without $\varepsilon$ moves from
a state in $\extended{\Delta}(q,\tau)$ with
$S=\Delta(q_{i_0},c)\cup\cdots \,\Delta(q_{i_k},c)$.
Then include $\varepsilon$ moves:
$
  \extended{\Delta}(q,\tau\concat c)=
  \extended{E}(s_0)\cup\extended{E}(s_1)\cup\cdots\,\extended{E}(s_n)
$ for $s_i\in S$.
Observe that the machine
$\FSM$ accepts~$\tau_0$
if $\extended{\Delta}(q_0,\tau_0)\intersection F$ is not empty and
otherwise $\FSM$ 
rejects~$\tau_0$.
%</disc:EpsilonClosureii>

\index{epsilon transitions@$\varepsilon$ transitions|)}




% ........................................
\subsection{Equivalence of the machine types}\label{sec:EquivDFSMAndNFSM}

In this section we have apparently extended the capabilities of
Finite State machines, including by 
adding nondeterminism.
This can be puzzling.
For instance, if we feed the input $\tau=\str{010101110}$ to the machine
from \cref{ex:ListenerMachine} then it accepts,
but that machine is set up to accept strings that begin with two
blocks of \str{01}'s, while~$\tau$ begins with three.
How can it know, without peeking ahead, that it should ignore
the first block but transition on the second?
How can it guess?

In mathematics
we can consider whatever we can make precise
but we have so far stuck to
models of machines that are in principle physically realizable.
So this may seem to be a shift. 

It is not a shift.
We finish this section by showing 
how to convert any nondeterministic Finite State
machine into a deterministic one that does the same job.
So we can take nondeterminism
to be an abbreviation, a shorthand, a convenience, a way of 
alternatively specifying a deterministic machine.
This obviates at least some of the paradox of guessing.

%<*th:dfsmEquivndfsm>
\begin{theorem}
  The class of languages recognized by nondeterministic Finite State machines
  equals the class of languages recognized by deterministic Finite State
  machines.
  This remains true if we allow the nondeterministic machines to
  have $\varepsilon$~transitions.
\end{theorem}
%</th:dfsmEquivndfsm>

%<*pf:dfsmEquivndfsm>
Inclusion in one direction is easy.
In a deterministic machine the next-state function outputs
single states and
to make it a nondeterministic machine, just convert those states
into singleton sets.
Thus the set of languages recognized by
deterministic machines is a subset of the set recognized by
nondeterministic machines.

We will demonstrate inclusion in the other direction constructively,
starting with nondeterministic machines and
building deterministic machines that recognize the same language.
The two examples below show the construction. 
%</pf:dfsmEquivndfsm>
Our complete description of the algorithm comes after the 
first example.
We won't give a proof that this construction works simply because
the examples are entirely convincing.

\begin{example}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!convert to a deterministic machine} \label{ex:NondeterminsiticMachineWithoutEpsilon}
This nondeterministic machine $\FSM_N$
has no $\varepsilon$ transitions.
Its language is 
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}
     \suchthat\text{$\sigma=\str{a}^n$ or $\sigma=\str{a}^n\str{ab}$ for $n\in\N$}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm16.pdf}
\end{center}
The associated deterministic machine $\FSM_D$
is below. 
Each member of this machine is a set of $\FSM_N$'s states.
The start state of $\FSM_D$ is the one-element set $\set{q_0}=s_1$ containing
the start state of $\FSM_N$, and
a state of $\FSM_D$ is accepting
if any of its elements are accepting states in $\FSM_N$.

As an illustration of constructing the transition table,
suppose that we are in $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$ and are 
reading~\str{a}.
Combine the above machine's next states due to~$q_0$
with those due to~$q_2$, that is,
$\Delta_D(s_5,\str{a})=\set{q_0,q_1}\union\set{}=\set{q_0,q_1}$.
That's~$s_4$.
\begin{center}\small
  \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
      &\str{a}   &\str{b}         \\
      \cline{2-3}
    $s_0=\set{}$                           &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_1=\set{q_0}$         &$s_4$  &$s_0$  \\
    $s_2=\set{q_1}$                         &$s_0$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_3=\set{q_2}$         &$s_0$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_4=\set{q_0,q_1}$     &$s_4$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$     &$s_4$  &$s_0$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$      &$s_0$  &$s_3$  \\
    \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  &$s_4$  &$s_3$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
  \quad
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm17.pdf}}
\end{center}
The machine's table, and its transition graph, make clear that
$\FSM_D$ is deterministic.
Many of the states are unreachable; for example, $s_6$ 
has only outgoing arrows.
Below is the machine with those states removed.
Again, the start state is $s_1$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1050.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{example}

%<*df:DFSMFromNFSM>
Now we give the algorithm, the 
\definend{powerset construction}.\index{powerset construction}\index{Finite State machine!powerset construction}\index{nondeterministic Finite State machine!convert to a deterministic machine}
%  for constructing a 
% deterministic machine~$\FSM_D$ with the same behavior
% as a nondeterministic machine~$\FSM_N$,
% whether or not $\FSM_N$ has $\varepsilon$~transitions.
States in $\FSM_D$ are sets of states from~$\FSM_N$.
The start state of $\FSM_D$ is the  $\varepsilon$~closure $\hat{E}(q_0)$
(for machines without $\varepsilon$ moves
this is $\set{q_0}$).
A state of $\FSM_D$ is accepting if it  
contains any of $\FSM_N$'s accepting states.

The transition function $\Delta_D$ inputs
a state $s_i\in\FSM_D$, that is, 
$s_i=\set{q_{k_0},\ldots\, q_{k_i}}$, 
along with a character~$c\in\Sigma$. 
First apply $\FSM_N$'s next state function to $s_i$'s elements to get a set
$S_{i,c}=\Delta_{N}(q_{k_0},c)\cup\cdots\, \Delta_{N}(q_{k_i},c)$
(if $s_i$ is empty then $S_{i,c}$ is also empty).
Then include $\varepsilon$ moves:~where 
$S_{i,c}=\set{q_{j_0}, \ldots\, q_{j_i}}$, 
let 
$\Delta_D(s_i,c)=\hat{E}(q_{j_0})\cup\cdots\, \hat{E}(q_{j_i})$.
(For machines without $\varepsilon$ transitions this second part has no effect.)
%</df:DFSMFromNFSM>

\begin{example}  \label{ex:NFSMEpsilonClosure}
We next do a nondeterministic machine
with $\varepsilon$ transitions.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm18.pdf}
\end{center}
The table below computes
the associated deterministic machine.
The start state is $\hat{E}(q_0)=\set{q_0,q_3}=s_7$.
A state is accepting if it contains $q_1$. 

Here is an example walking through the powerset algorithm. 
First, let the machine be in state $s_7=\set{q_0,q_3}$
and reading~\str{b}.
In the terms of the algorithm's description, 
applying $\Delta_N$ to each element of $s_7$ gives 
$S_{7,\str{b}}
  =\Delta_N(q_0,\str{b})\union\Delta_N(q_3,\str{b})=\set{q_1}\union\set{}
  =\set{q_1}$.
Taking the $\varepsilon$ closure gives
$\Delta_D(s_7,\str{b})=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}=s_{12}$.

Finding the $\varepsilon$ closures in advance
is a help in constructing the table.
We have $\hat{E}(q_0)=\set{q_0,q_3}=s_7$,
and  $\hat{E}(q_1)=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}=s_{12}$,
and  $\hat{E}(q_2)=\set{q_2}=s_3$,
and  $\hat{E}(q_3)=\set{q_3}=s_4$.

%<*ex:DFSMFromNFSMWithEpsilon>
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{2}{c}|*{2}{c}}
   \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
    &$S_{i,\str{a}}$\rule{0pt}{12pt}  
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\Delta_D(s_i,\str{a})$}   
    &$S_{i,\str{b}}$ 
     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\Delta_D(s_i,\str{b})$}   
    \\
    \cline{2-5}
  $s_0=\set{}$        &$\set{}$   &$s_0$  
                          &$\set{}$  &$s_0$  \\
  $s_1=\set{q_0}$     &$\set{q_2}$   &$s_{3}$ 
                          &$\set{q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_2=\set{q_1}$      &$\set{}$   &$s_0$  
                          &$\set{}$  &$s_0$  \\
  $s_3=\set{q_2}$     &$\set{}$       &$s_0$  
                        &$\set{q_0}$    &$s_7$  \\
  $s_4=\set{q_3}$    &$\set{q_3}$  &$s_4$  
                        &$\set{}$     &$s_0$  \\[1ex]
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_5=\set{q_0,q_1}$   &$\set{q_2}$  &$s_{3}$  
                          &$\set{q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  $s_6=\set{q_0,q_2}$   &$\set{q_2}$    &$s_{3}$  
                          &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  $s_7=\set{q_0,q_3}$   &$\set{q_2,q_3}$   &$s_{10}$
                          &$\set{q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_8=\set{q_1,q_2}$   &$\set{}$   &$s_{0}$  
                          &$\set{q_0}$  &$s_{7}$  \\
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_9=\set{q_1,q_3}$    &$\set{q_3}$        &$s_{4}$  
                           &$\set{}$  &$s_{0}$  \\
  $s_{10}=\set{q_2,q_3}$   &$\set{q_3}$  &$s_4$                                 
                            &$\set{q_0}$    &$s_7$  \\[1ex]
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_{11}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  &$\set{q_2}$   &$s_{3}$  
                               &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_{12}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$   &$\set{q_2,q_3}$     &$s_{10}$  
                                &$\set{q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  $s_{13}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_3}$   &$\set{q_2,q_3}$  &$s_{10}$  
                                &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_{14}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_3}$   &$\set{q_3}$   &$s_{4}$  
                                &$\set{q_0}$  &$s_{7}$  \\
  \acceptingstate 
  $s_{15}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_3}$ &$\set{q_2,q_3}$    &$s_{10}$  
                                 &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  &$s_{12}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
%</ex:DFSMFromNFSMWithEpsilon>
The transition diagram is below.
Many of the machine's table's sixteen states,
are unreachable from the starting state~$s_7=\hat{E}(q_0)$.
We can see that by starting at~$s_7$ and tracing through the states.
The diagram omits unreachable states.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1043.pdf}  
\end{center}
\end{example} 

The powerset construction shows that 
for any nondeterministic machine there is a deterministic machine
that recognizes the same language.

We can say more:~if the nondeterministic machine
has $n$ states then the
deterministic machine has at most $2^n$ states.
(It turns out that $2^n$ is the best that we can do in that for 
any $n$ there is an $n$~state nondeterministic machine requiring
a deterministic machine with $2^n$ states.
However, in practice usually the deterministic machine
is not too big once we minimize the number of states.
\Cref{sec:Minimization} shows how to minimize.)


% .....................................
\begin{exercises}
  % \hspace*{0.7em}\textit{Unless specified otherwise, you can use
  %   $\varepsilon$ transitions in the nondeterministic machines
  %   in these exercises.}
\begin{exercise}
Give the transition function for the machine of
\cref{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}, and of \cref{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The main point is that entries in the table are sets of states.
  On the left is the machine from \cref{ex:NFSMSubstringAAOrBB}
  while \cref{ex:NFSMRepeatsofAC}'s machine is on the right.
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
                               &\str{a}           &\str{b}   \\
      \cline{2-3}
                      $q_0$    &$\set{q_0,q_{1}}$ &$\set{q_0,q_2}$   \\
                      $q_1$    &$\set{q_3}$       &$\set{}$   \\
                      $q_2$    &$\set{}$          &$\set{q_3}$   \\
      \acceptingstate $q_3$    &$\set{q_3}$       &$\set{q_3}$   \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
    \qquad
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
                               &\str{a}       &\str{b}   &\str{c}      \\
      \cline{2-4}
      \acceptingstate $q_0$    &$\set{q_{1}}$ &$\set{}$  &$\set{}$     \\
                      $q_1$    &$\set{}$      &$\set{}$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Consider this machine.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm30.pdf}
  \end{center}
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item Does it accept the empty string?
  \item The string \str{0}?
  \item \str{011}?
  \item \str{010}?
  % \item Are all strings of the form $\str{1}^j$ accepted, for $j>0$?
  \item List all length five accepted strings.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Yes.
  From $q_0$ an $\varepsilon$~transition goes to $q_2$, which is an accepting
  state.
  That is, an accepting branch is
  $\sequence{q_0,\varepsilon}\yields\sequence{q_2,\varepsilon}$,
  where the transition there is an $\varepsilon$ transition. 
  \item No.
  From $q_0$ the machine can either read the~\str{0} and go to
  $q_1$, or take the $\varepsilon$~transition
  to $q_2$ and then read the~\str{0}, which sends the machine to 
  no-state.
  Neither $q_2$ nor no-state is an accepting state.

  Said more formally, the $\varepsilon$~closure of $q_0$ is $\set{q_0,q_2}$.
  Neither state, on reading the character~\str{0}, transitions to a state
  whose $\varepsilon$~closure includes an accepting state. 
  \item Yes.
  With that input the machine can go from $q_0$ to~$q_1$, then to~$q_2$,
  and then around the loop to~$q_2$ again.
  More formally, this is a sequence of allowed transitions that ends in an
  accepting state.
  \begin{equation*}
    \sequence{q_0,\str{011}}
    \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{11}}
    \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{1}}
    \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
  \end{equation*}
  \item No.
  Starting at~$q_0$ with the character~\str{0} first on the tape, the machine
  can do two things.
  If it takes the $\varepsilon$~transition then when it reads the~\str{0}
  it goes to no-state, which is not an accepting state.

  Otherwise, the~\str{0} moves the machine to~$q_1$.
  Next, on the following tape character~\str{1}, 
  the machine moves to~$q_2$.
  Finally, on the third character~\str{0},
  the machine moves to no-state. 
  \item There are two: \str{01111} and~\str{11111}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Your class has someone who asks, 
``I get that it is interesting, but 
isn't all this machine-guessing stuff just mathematical
abstractions that are not real?''
How might the prof respond?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Someone in this class saying that a topic
  is ``just mathematical abstraction'' is
  like someone objecting in an aerodynamics class that this is all 
  ``just air.''
  Yes, that's what we are doing.

  Said another way, if someone compares
  the thinking about computation that we are doing here with 
  the thinking about computatoin that happens in a course on data structures, 
  and holds the latter up as a model of ``real'' 
  then that is a stretch.
  ``Real'' is changing diapers, or grafting fruit trees,
  or feeding the hungry.
  All of what we are doing 
  is abstraction of one kind of another.

  If that person responds, ``OK but abstraction can go too far
  and get away from what brought us into the program'' then
  that is more measured.
  % One answer is that people in a program need to take as the default assumption 
  % that the people who put together this program,
  % and such programs across the world, have put in steps that lead somewhere.
  One reason that nondeterminism is here, among many, 
  is because it is necessary to understand
  the material of the fifth chapter, which describes the most 
  important problem in the field today, which will directly impact
  all practitioners, the $\P$ versus~$\NP$ question.

  Besides, it is fun.
  Surely that merits at least some consideration.  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Your friend objects, ``Epsilon transitions don't make any sense
  because the machine below will never get its first
  step done; it just endlessly follows the epsilons.''
  Correct their misimpression.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm59.pdf}
  \end{center}
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Going around forever in a cycle between $q_0$ and $q_1$,
    always taking the $\varepsilon$ transistions, will indeed
    not lead to a successful outcome.
    But the definition does not require that 
    there must not be any wrong ways to process
    the input string, only that there must be a right way.

    The machine accepts the input string  
    if there is a sequence of transitions 
    that has a halting configuration with an 
    accepting state.
    The given machine 
    can accept \str{aab} by starting in $q_0$,
    transitioning to $q_1$ on the first \str{a},
    taking the $\varepsilon$ transition to~$q_0$,
    then going to $q_1$ on the second~\str{a}, 
    then going to $q_2$, which is accepting, with the~\str{b}.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{q_0,\str{aab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{ab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{ab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{b}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
    \end{equation*}
    % This machine recognizes the language
    % $\lang=\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}\suchthat n\in\N}$.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a sequence of `$\yields$' relations showing that 
\cref{ex:ListenerMachine}'s machine accepts $\tau=\str{010101110}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
This is the sequence of transitions.
\begin{multline*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{010101110}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{10101110}}
  \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{0101110}}
  \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{101110}}     \\
  \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{01110}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_3,\str{1110}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_4,\str{110}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_5,\str{10}}    
  \yields \sequence{q_6,\str{0}}
  \yields \sequence{q_7,\emptystring}
\end{multline*}  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:MFSMWithEpsilonTrans}
This machine has $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm19.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item What is the $\varepsilon$ closure of $q_0$?
Of $q_1$?
$q_2$?
\item Does it accept the empty string?
\item \str{a}?
  \str{b}?  
\item Show that it accepts \str{aab} by producing a suitable sequence
  of $\yields$ relations.
\item List five strings of minimal length that it accepts.
\item List five of minimal length that it does not accept.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Here are the $\varepsilon$ closures.
\begin{display}
\begin{tabular}{r|*{3}{c}}
  \tabulartext{state $q$} &$q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\
  \cline{2-4}
  \tabulartext{$\varepsilon$ closure $\hat{E}(q)$}
                      &$\set{q_0}$ &$\set{q_1,q_2}$  &$\set{q_2}$
\end{tabular}
\end{display}
  \item It does not accept the empty string because the $\varepsilon$~closure
  of $q_0$ does not contain any final states.
  \item It accepts both of the one-character strings \str{a} and~\str{b}.
  For instance, on the string~\str{a} the machine can transition 
  from $q_0$ to~$q_1$,
  and then it can make an $\varepsilon$~transition to~$q_2$, which is 
  an accepting state.
  \item The given machine 
    can accept \str{aab} by starting in $q_0$,
    transitioning to $q_0$ on the first \str{a},
    then going to $q_1$ on the second~\str{a}, 
    taking the $\varepsilon$ transition to~$q_2$,
    then going to $q_2$ with the~\str{b}, thereby ending in an
    accepting state.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{q_0,\str{aab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_0,\str{ab}}
      \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{b}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{b}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
    \end{equation*}
  \item Five of the shortest strings are: \str{a}, \str{b}, \str{aa}, \str{ab},
  and~\str{aab}.
  \item Five that it does not accept are:~the empty string~$\emptystring$,
  \str{ba}, \str{baa}, \str{bab}, and~\str{baaa}.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Produce the table description of the next-state function $\Delta$
  for the machine in the prior exercise.
  It should have three columns, for \str{a}, \str{b}, and $\varepsilon$.
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta]
                             &\str{a}        &\str{b}         &$\varepsilon$ \\
                             \cline{2-4}
                       $q_0$ &$\set{q_0,q_1}$ &$\set{q_{1}}$   &$\set{}$   \\
                       $q_1$ &$\set{}$       &$\set{}$        &$\set{q_2}$  \\
       \acceptingstate $q_2$ &$\set{}$       &$\set{q_0,q_2}$  &$\set{}$  
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Consider this machine.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm29.pdf}
  \end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item  Show that it accepts \str{011} by producing a suitable sequence
  of $\yields$ relations.
% \item What happens if you try this as a first step of that sequence? 
%     \begin{equation*}
%       \sequence{q_0,\str{011}}
%       \yields \sequence{q_1,\str{11}}
%     \end{equation*}  
\item Show that the machine accepts \str{00011} by producing a suitable sequence
  of $\yields$ relations.
\item
Does it accept the empty string?
\item \str{0}?
  \str{1}?  
\item
List five strings of minimal length that it accepts.
\item 
List five of minimal length that it does not accept.
\item 
What is the language of this machine?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item $
      \sequence{q_0,\str{011}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{11}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\str{1}}
      \yields \sequence{q_2,\emptystring}     
    $  
% \item
\item
$
\sequence{q_0,\str{00011}}
\yields
\sequence{q_1,\str{0011}}
\yields
\sequence{q_1,\str{011}}
\yields
\sequence{q_1,\str{11}}
\yields
\sequence{q_2,\str{1}}
\yields
\sequence{q_2,\emptystring}
$
\item Yes, because the state $q_0$ is accepting.
\item
It accepts \str{0} because it can go through these steps that
end in an accepting state.
\begin{equation*}
  \sequence{q_0,\str{0}}
\yields
\sequence{q_2,\emptystring}
\end{equation*}
It does not accept \str{1} because there is no such sequence of steps.
\item 
Five are
\emptystring, \str{0}, \str{01}, \str{011}, and \str{0111}.
\item
Here are five:
\str{1}, \str{10}, \str{11}, \str{010}, and~\str{110}.
\item
One description is
    $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
      \text{$\sigma=\str{0}^i\str{1}^j$ for $i\geq 1$ and~$j\geq 0$}}$.
(Note that $\sigma=\emptystring$ when $i=j=0$.)
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

Find the $\varepsilon$~closures of the states
of this nondeterministic machine,
using a table like the \cref{ex:FindEpsilonClosureInSteps}'s.

and $q_3$ reaches its limit at~$1$.

      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$1$}

      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$3$}  
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$\hat{E}(q)$}  \\

     $q_0$ &$\set{q_0}$ &$\set{q_0,q_1}$ &$\set{q_0,q_1}$

\begin{exercise} % Sipser p 81
Give diagrams for nondeterministic Finite State machines that
recognize the given language and that have the given number of states.
Use $\Sigma=\B$.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang_0=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ ends in \str{00}}}$, 
  having three states
\item $\lang_1=\set{\sigma\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ has the substring \str{0110}}}$,
  with five states
\item $\lang_2=\set{\sigma\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ contains an even number of \str{0}'s or exactly two \str{1}'s}}$, with six states

\begin{exercise}
  What is the language of this nondeterministic machine with
  $\varepsilon$ transitions?
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1014.pdf}
  \end{center}
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Any \str{b} must be followed by an~\str{a}, except if it appears
    at the end of the string, where it must be a string of \str{b}'s
    without an~\str{a}.
    In short, the language is 
    $\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat
      \text{$\sigma$ has no substring \str{bba}}}$.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}

  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_{D_0}]

                      \cline{2-3}

                      $s_1=\set{q_0}$                 &$s_4$    &$s_0$  \\
                      $s_2=\set{q_1}$                 &$s_3$    &$s_3$  \\

                      $s_4=\set{q_0, q_1}$            &$s_7$    &$s_3$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$             &$s_4$    &$s_{1}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$             &$s_3$    &$s_{5}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_7=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$         &$s_7$    &$s_{5}$  \\

    \qquad

                                                      &\str{0}  &\str{1}  \\

                      $s_2=\set{q_1}$                 &$s_3$    &$s_3$  \\

                      $s_4=\set{q_0, q_1}$            &$s_7$    &$s_3$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_5=\set{q_0,q_2}$             &$s_7$    &$s_{5}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_1,q_2}$             &$s_3$    &$s_{5}$  \\

\begin{exercise} % Taylor p 511
This table
\begin{center}
  \begin{transitionfunction}
            &\str{a}      &\str{b}         \\
    \cline{2-3}
    $q_0$   &$\set{q_0}$  &$\set{q_1,q_2}$  \\
    $q_1$   &$\set{q_3}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    $q_2$   &$\set{q_1}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\
    \acceptingstate $q_3$   &$\set{q_3}$  &$\set{q_3}$  \\
  \end{transitionfunction}
\end{center}
gives the next-state function for a nondeterministic Finite State machine.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Draw the transition graph.
\item What is the recognized language?
\item Give the next-state table for a deterministic machine that recognizes the
  same language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  The alphabet is $\Sigma=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item \
  \begin{display}
      \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm1000.pdf}}
  \end{display}
  \item A string is in the language if it has the form
    $\sigma_0\concat\sigma_1\concat\sigma_2$ where 
    $\sigma_0=\str{a}^k\str{b}$ for some $k\in\N$
    and $\sigma_1$ is either \str{aa} or \str{ab} or \str{b},
    and $\sigma_2$ is any member of $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$
  \item This is the transition function for the associated deterministic 
  machine. 
  \begin{display}
    \begin{transitionfunction}[\Delta_D]
                                                      &\str{a}  &\str{b}  \\
                      \cline{2-3}
                      $s_0=\set{}$                    &$s_0$    &$s_0$  \\
                      $s_1=\set{q_0}$                 &$s_1$    &$s_8$  \\
                      $s_2=\set{q_1}$                 &$s_4$    &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_3=\set{q_2}$                 &$s_2$    &$s_4$  \\
                      $s_4=\set{q_3}$                 &$s_4$    &$s_4$  \\[1ex]
                      $s_5=\set{q_0,q_1}$             &$s_7$    &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_6=\set{q_0,q_2}$             &$s_5$    &$s_{14}$  \\
                      $s_7=\set{q_0,q_3}$             &$s_7$    &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_8=\set{q_1,q_2}$             &$s_9$    &$s_4$  \\
                      $s_9=\set{q_1,q_3}$             &$s_4$    &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{10}=\set{q_2,q_3}$          &$s_9$     &$s_4$  \\[1ex]
      \acceptingstate $s_{11}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$      &$s_{12}$  &$s_{14}$  \\
                      $s_{12}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$      &$s_7$     &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{13}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_{12}$  &$s_{14}$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{14}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_3}$      &$s_9$     &$s_4$  \\
      \acceptingstate $s_{15}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_3}$  &$s_{12}$  &$s_{14}$  \\
    \end{transitionfunction}
  \end{display}
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A grammar is 
\definend{right linear}\index{right linear}\index{grammar!right linear}\index{grammar!linear} 
if every production rule has the
form \mbox{\ntrm{N}\produces \trm{x}\ntrm{M}}, where the right side has
a single terminal followed by a single nonterminal.
With this right linear grammar
we can associate this nondeterministic Finite State machine.
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{1.25in}
\begin{grammar}
S \produces{} \trm{a} A

A \produces{} \trm{a} A \syntaxor \trm{b} B

B \produces{} \trm{b} B \syntaxor b
\end{grammar}
\end{minipage}
\hspace*{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/nfsm/nfsm68.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Give three strings from the language of the grammar
and show that they are accepted by the machine.
\item Describe that language.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
  \begin{exparts}
  \item This is a derivation of \str{abb}.
    \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}[t]{l}
      S
        \derives \trm{a}A 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b} 
    \end{tabular}       
    \end{display}
    This is a derivation of \str{aabb}.
    \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}[t]{l}
      S 
        \derives \trm{a}A
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{a}A 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{a}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b} 
    \end{tabular}       
    \end{display}
    And, this is a derivation of \str{abbb}.
    \begin{display}
    \begin{tabular}[t]{l}
      S 
        \derives \trm{a}A 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b}B 
        \derives \trm{a}\trm{b}\trm{b}\trm{b} 
    \end{tabular}       
    \end{display}
    This verifies that the first is accepted by the machine.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{S,\str{abb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{bb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{b}}
      \yields\sequence{F,\emptystring}
    \end{equation*}
    The second is here.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{S,\str{aabb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{abb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{bb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{b}}
      \yields\sequence{F,\emptystring}
    \end{equation*}
    The third is similar.
    \begin{equation*}
      \sequence{S,\str{abbb}}
      \yields\sequence{A,\str{bbb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{bb}}
      \yields\sequence{B,\str{b}}
      \yields\sequence{F,\emptystring}
    \end{equation*}
  \item The language of the machine and the grammar is 
    $\lang=\set{\str{a}^i\str{b}^j\suchthat i>0, j>1}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Decide whether each problem is solvable or
unsolvable by a Turing machine.
\begin{exparts}
\item $\lang_{DFA}=\set{\sequence{\FSM,\sigma}\suchthat
         \text{the deterministic Finite State machine $\FSM$ accepts $\sigma$}}$
\item $\lang_{NFA}=\set{\sequence{\FSM,\sigma}\suchthat
         \text{the nondeterministic machine $\FSM$ accepts $\sigma$}}$
\end{exparts}
% (Assume that $\FSM$ is described by using bitstrings 
% to encode the transition function.)
\begin{answer}\verified
  Each of these is solvable.  
  For each we will sketch an algorithm.
  \begin{exparts}
  \item Clearly we can write a simulator for deterministic Finite
  State machines.
  This simulator is uniform in that it takes in the $\Delta$ table and
  the input string.
  By Church's Thesis since we can write it on a modern computer, we
  can write one for a Turing machine.
  When given an input~$\sigma$, the machine will consume it in
  no more than $\sizeof{\sigma}$ transitions, and so 
  the simulation is sure to halt.
  Then we can just check whether the ending state is an accepting state.
  \item   
  The section body discusses how to simulate a nondeterministic computation,
  by dovetailing (that is, time-slicing).
  Or, we can use the ppowerset algorithm to convert it to a deterministic 
  machine and then simulate that.
  We can then answer questions about whether this machine accepts 
  a given input~$\sigma$ just as in the prior item.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
