states are $0$-indistinguishable if either both are accepting states or
both are not accepting states.

\begin{example}
Consider the machine on the left in ($*$)
above and its $s_0$ and $s_2$.
Take the length one string $\sigma=\str{0}$.
Starting in state~$s_0$
and processing~$\sigma$ ends
in the rejecting state~$s_1$,
but starting in~$s_2$ and processing the same input ends
in the accepting state~$s_3$.
So $s_0$ and~$s_2$ are $1$-distinguishable and therefore are
distinguishable.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:MinimizationInitial}
For the machine on the right above
this table gives the result of starting in each state and processing
each string of length~$0$, length~$1$, and length~$2$.
\begin{equation*}\small
  \begin{array}{r|c*{2}{c}*{4}{c}}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\emptystring}  
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}           
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}           
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{00}}           
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{01}}           
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{10}}           
           &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{11}}           
            \\ \cline{2-8}
    q_0  &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{R}  \\
    q_1  &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A}  \\
    q_2  &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{R}  \\
    q_3  &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A}  \\
    q_4  &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{R} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{R}  \\
    q_5  &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A} &\mathrm{A}  \\      
  \end{array}
  % \tag{$**$}
\end{equation*}
The initial column just records that
members of $\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}$ 
are rejecting states while members of $\set{q_3,q_5}$ are accepting. 
We write $\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}$ and 
$\eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_3,q_5}$ to 
denote that states are in the same class $\eclass_{0,i}$ if they are
$0$-indistinguishable.

The two columns for the length~$1$ strings
show all four possible behaviors.
The state $q_0$ goes to a rejecting result for both inputs,
that is, the columns say R and~R.
For $q_1$ we have R,A.
Both $q_2$ and~$q_4$ show A,R.
And $q_3$ and~$q_5$  go to A,A. 
So there are four classes for 
$1$-indistinguishability: $\eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}$,
$\eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}$, 
$\eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}$, 
and $\eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}$.
Note that the prior paragraph's
class $\eclass_{0,1}$ has split into three,
$\eclass_{1,0}$ and $\eclass_{1,1}$ and~$\eclass_{1,2}$.
The other class did not split, $\eclass_{0,1}=\eclass_{1,3}$.

Next, the length~$2$ strings.
The only classes that could split are $\eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}$, 
and $\eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}$. 
Both $q_2$ and~$q_4$ have
A,A,A,R,
while both $q_3$ and~$q_5$ have
A,A,A,A.
So going to these longer strings   
does not further divide the classes.
% \Dash
% the
% relation of $2$-distinguishable gives the same classes
% as does the relation of $1$-distinguishable.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma} \label{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel}
  The relations $\sim_0$, $\sim_1$, \ldots{} and $\sim$
  are equivalences.
  As such, they partition a machine's states into
  the  
  \definend{$0$-distinguishable classes},\index{n-distinguishable classes@$n$-distinguishable classes}
  the \definend{$1$-distinguishable classes},
  \ldots{}
  along with the \definend{distinguishable classes}.\index{distinguishable classes}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \cref{exer:NEquivIsEquivRel}.  
\end{proof}

This summarizes the prior example by listing the equivalence classes.
\begin{equation*}
  \begin{array}{r|l}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{n}
  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{$\sim_{n}$ classes}}
  \\ \hline
  0
  &\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_3,q_5}
  \\
  1
  &\eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}
  \\
  2
  &\eclass_{2,0}=\set{q_0}
  \quad
  \eclass_{2,1}=\set{q_1}
  \quad
  \eclass_{2,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}
  \quad
  \eclass_{2,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}
  \end{array}
\end{equation*}
When we first saw this machine we 
spotted that $q_2$ and~$q_4$ are redundant.
Notice that all three lines in this table have them together in their class. 
The same holds for $q_3$ and~$q_5$.

We now develop an algorithm, \citetext{Moore's algorithm}{%
  It is easy and suitable for
  small calculations but if you are writing code then 
  be aware that another algorithm,
    Hopcroft's algorithm, is more efficient.
  See \protect\autocite{Knuutila}.},\index{Moore's algorithm}\index{minimization of a Finite State machine!Moore's algorithm}
based on this pattern.
Given a machine, it finds the $\sim_0$ classes, 
then the $\sim_1$ classes, 
\ldots{}
until the classes stop splitting.
At that point the classes are the $\sim$~classes,
and they serve as the states of the minimal machine.

There is just one difficulty to overcome.
As presented in \cref{ex:MinimizationInitial},
at each stage we considered all length~$n$ string inputs.
Of course there are $2^n$-many such inputs to check,
which would make an algorithm intolerably slow.
Moore's algorithm refines that example,
reducing the number of things to check.

For that, consider
what we need for a splitting, that is,
consider two states $q$ and~$\hat{q}$ that are not
$n$-distinguishable but that are $n+1$-distinguishable.
Let them be distinguished by the length~$n+1$ string 
$\sigma=\sequence{s_0,s_1,\,\ldots s_{n-1},s_n}$, which we can 
write as $\sigma=\tau\concat s_n$.
Because they are not $n$-distinguishable,
where $\tau$ brings the machine from $q$ to some state~$r$
and also brings the machine from $\hat{q}$ to some~$\hat{r}$,
then $r$ and~$\hat{r}$ are in the same $n$-equivalence
class,~$\eclass_{n,i}$.
Therefore, the distinguishing between these two states
must happen with $\sigma$'s final character,~$s_n$.
It must take the machine from state~$r$ to a state in one 
class,~$\eclass_{n+1,j}$, 
and also take the machine from~$\hat{r}$ to 
a state in a different class,~$\eclass_{n+1,\hat{j}}\,$.
In short, if there is going to be a split in passing from the 
$n$-distinguishability classes
to the $n+1$-distinguishability classes 
then a single character suffices to produce such a split.

% So consider again the machine on the right in ($*$).
% Its $\sim_1$ classes are 
% $\eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0}$, 
% $\eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1}$, 
% $\eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_2,q_4}$, 
% and~$\eclass_{1,3}=\set{q_3,q_5}$.
% We already know that there will be no  
% additional splitting in going to the $\sim_2$ classes, 
% but we will illustrate how the algorithm checks this. 
% We feed single characters to the states in the $\sim_1$~classes
% and see if those classes split.
% This is a considerable speedup over checking all possible strings.
% (And singleton classes cannot split so we need only test classes with 
% more than one member,
% $\eclass_{1,2}$ and $\eclass_{1,3}$.)
% \begin{center}
%   \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\eclass_{1,2}$}
%          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} \\
%     \cline{2-3}
%     $q_2$    &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,3}$ &$q_4\in\eclass_{1,2}$      \\
%     $q_4$    &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,3}$ &$q_4\in\eclass_{1,2}$      
%   \end{tabular}
%   \qquad  
%   \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\eclass_{1,3}$}
%          &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} \\
%     \cline{2-3}
%     $q_3$   &$q_3\in\eclass_{1,3}$ &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,3}$    \\
%     $q_5$   &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,3}$ &$q_5\in\eclass_{1,3}$    
%   \end{tabular}
% \end{center}
% The $\eclass_{1,2}$ table shows both $q_2$ and~$q_4$ 
% going to $\eclass_{1,3},\eclass_{1,2}$
% so there is no split there.
% The $\eclass_{1,3}$ likewise shows no split.
% So they will never split and algorithm stops\Dash the 
% $\sim_1$ classes equal the $\sim_2$ classes, \ldots{},
% which equal the $\sim$ classes.

The examples of Moore's algorithm that follow leverage this reasoning.
They also show how to use the $\sim$~classes to make 
a minimal machine.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:FirstMinimization}
  We will find a machine that recognizes the same language as this one but
  that has a minimum number of states.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min03.pdf}
  \end{center}
  For bookkeeping we will use triangular tables that
  have an entry for every pair of different states $q_i$ and~$q_j$.
  Checkmarks show state pairs that are distinguishable.

  Start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries that are 
  $0$-distinguishable, that is,
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  For example, the table below 
  has a check denoting that state~$q_0$
  is $0$-distinguishable from state~$q_1$.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &    &    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{center}
   Blank entries in this initial table 
   mark state pairs that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   For instance, $q_1$ and~$q_2$ are $0$-indistinguishable.
   By eye we can read off the $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_3,q_4}
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1,q_2,q_5}        
   \end{equation*}

  Next the algorithm determines whether these classes split.
  We check each pair $q_i,q_j$ that are together in a $\sim_{0}$~class.
  We take the characters from the alphabet and see where that character
  sends $q_i$ and~$q_j$.
  We see splitting when they are sent to members of unequal
  $\sim_{0}$~classes.
  For instance, on the $q_0,q_3$ line, the character~\str{a} takes
  $q_0$ to~$q_1$ and takes $q_3$ to~$q_5$.
  The two outcomes, $q_1$ and~$q_5$, are both members of $\eclass_{0,1}$,
  so we have not yet seen splitting.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_2,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_0,q_4$  &$q_1,q_5$      &$q_2,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_5,q_5$       &$q_5,q_5$                              
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}       
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_3,q_4$                    &$q_4,q_3$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$     &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_5$}  &$q_3,q_5$     &$q_4,q_5$   
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$     &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$ \\
      \splitclasses{$q_2,q_5$}  &$q_4,q_5$     &$q_3,q_5$   
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$     &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$ \\
    \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  But the table does show class splitting.
  One example is in the $q_1,q_5$ line. 
  Those two states are together in $\eclass_{0,1}$
  but on the right the entry under~\str{a} has two different classes, 
  $\eclass_{0,0}$ and~$\eclass_{0,1}$.
  Therefore at least one of the $\sim_{0}$~classes splits and  
  the algorithm goes on to find $\sim_{1}$~classes.   
    
  We refine that triangular table to make the next one.
  Because of the $q_1,q_5$ splitting in the prior paragraph
  we add a checkmark in location $1,5$.
  Similarly, the prior table shows a $q_2,q_5$ splitting so
  we add a checkmark at $2,5$.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  We can read off the  $\sim_{1}$~classes.
  \begin{equation*}
    \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0,q_3,q_4} \quad
    \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1,q_2}   \quad
    \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_5}   
  \end{equation*}
  The $\sim_0$ class $\eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_5}$ has split
  into two $\sim_1$~classes. 

  Now iterate.
  Obviously $\eclass_{1,2}$ can't split so we only consider state pairs
  from the other two classes.
  The right side shows that $q_0$ is $2$-distinguishable from $q_3$ and~$q_4$. 
  The next triangular table adds 
  checkmarks in the $0,3$ and $0,4$ entries.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$\set{q_0,q_3}$}  &$\set{q_1,q_5}$   &$\set{q_2,q_5}$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,2}$     &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$\set{q_0,q_4}$}  &$\set{q_1,q_5}$  &$\set{q_2,q_5}$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,2}$     &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      $\set{q_3,q_4}$  &$\set{q_5,q_5}$   &$\set{q_5,q_5}$  
             &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$     &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}                              
      $\set{q_1,q_2}$  &$\set{q_3,q_4}$  &$\set{q_4,q_3}$  
             &$\eclass_{1,0},\eclass_{1,0}$     &$\eclass_{1,0},\eclass_{1,0}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  That is, the $\sim_1$ class $\eclass_{1,0}$ splits into two $\sim_2$ classes.
  \begin{equation*}
        \eclass_{2,0}=\set{q_0} \quad
        \eclass_{2,1}=\set{q_1,q_2}   \quad
        \eclass_{2,2}=\set{q_3,q_4} \quad
        \eclass_{2,3}=\set{q_5}   
  \end{equation*}

  One more iteration gives this.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}                              
      $\set{q_1,q_2}$  &$\set{q_3,q_4}$  &$\set{q_4,q_3}$  
             &$\eclass_{2,2},\eclass_{2,2}$     &$\eclass_{2,2},\eclass_{2,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $\set{q_3,q_4}$  &$\set{q_5,q_5}$   &$\set{q_5,q_5}$  
             &$\eclass_{2,3},\eclass_{2,3}$     &$\eclass_{2,3},\eclass_{2,3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \qquad
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   &    \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck    &\ck    &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\ 
      \cline{1-5}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  That calculation shows no more splitting.
  The algorithm terminates.
  \begin{equation*}
        \eclass_0=\set{q_0} \quad
        \eclass_1=\set{q_1,q_2}   \quad
        \eclass_{2}=\set{q_3,q_4} \quad
        \eclass_{3}=\set{q_5}   
  \end{equation*}

  To finish we will produce the minimal machine.
  % The algorithm has found that states $q_1$
  % and~$q_2$ are redundant and that $q_3$ is redundant with~$q_4$
  % as well.
  % So we collapse those pairs. 
  %
  Take
  $r_0$ as a name for $\eclass_{0}$, 
  take $r_1$ for $\eclass_{1}$, 
  $r_2$ for $\eclass_{2}$, 
  and $r_3$ for $\eclass_{3}$.
  The start state is the one containing $q_0$,
  namely~$r_0$. 
  The final states are the ones containing final states of the
  original machine, $r_1$ and $r_3$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min04.pdf}
  \end{center}
  As to the transitions between states, 
  consider what happens when we feed the character~\str{a} to
  $r_0=\eclass_0=\set{q_0}$. 
  In the original machine, $q_0$ under input~\str{a} goes to~$q_1$.
  Since $q_1$ is an element of~$\eclass_{1}=r_1$, 
  in the minimal machine the \str{a}~arrow 
  out of $r_0$ goes to~$r_1$.
  Other connections work the same way.
\end{example}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SecondMinimization}
  We will minimize the machine below.
  The algorithm has an additional step, 
  which did not come up in the prior example
  but does come up here.
  This machine has an unreachable state,~$q_5$.
  So we start by omitting that state.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min00.pdf}
  \end{center}

  That leaves these $\sim_0$ classes
  and this initial triangular table.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
    \qquad
    $\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2} 
      \quad
     \eclass_{0,1}=\set{q_3,q_4}$
  \end{center}

  The algorithm calls for us to check whether the $\sim_0$~classes split.
  Below, the calculation's $q_0,q_1$ row shows  
  that on being fed a~\str{1} the
  outcomes, $q_2$ and~$q_3$,
  are in different $\sim_0$~classes,
  and its second row shows a similar thing. 
  We've included the updated triangular table.
  \begin{center} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_1$}  &$q_1,q_2$  &$q_2,q_3$ 
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$     &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_2$}  &$q_1,q_2$  &$q_2,q_4$ 
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$     &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_2,q_2$  &$q_3,q_4$
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$     &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$     &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
      \cline{1-4}      
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{center}
  That is, $\eclass_{0,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$ splits, and  
  these are the $\sim_1$~classes.
   \begin{equation*}
        \eclass_{1,0}=\set{q_0} \quad
        \eclass_{1,1}=\set{q_1,q_2} \quad
        \eclass_{1,2}=\set{q_3,q_4}
   \end{equation*}

  On the next iteration 
\begin{center}
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_2,q_2$  &$q_3,q_4$
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,1}$     &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$
             &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$     &$\eclass_{1,},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
\end{center}
no more splitting happens.
  The minimized machine has three states, one for each~$\sim$
  equivalence class,
  $\eclass_{0}=\set{q_0}$,
  $\eclass_{1}=\set{q_1,q_2}$,
  and $\eclass_{2}=\set{q_3,q_4}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min05.pdf}
  \end{center}
\end{example}

We end with a lemma verifying that Moore's algorithm works.
We will cover that the
algorithm halts, 
as well as that the machine it produces recognizes the same language
and is minimal.

But there is another thing to verify that may not be obvious,
namely that the algorithm produces a machine whose 
$\Delta$ function is well-defined.
Consider the minimal machine in \cref{ex:FirstMinimization} and the arrows
leaving its state $r_1=\eclass_1=\set{q_1,q_2}$.
What if feeding an~\str{a} to $q_1$ and $q_2$ 
sends them to states in different $\sim$~classes?
That would be trouble\Dash which state~$r_j$ should get the 
\str{a} arrow leaving $r_1$?
Now, \cref{ex:FirstMinimization} doesn't have this trouble.
Its states
$q_1$ and~$q_s$ are both sent by \str{a} to states in $\eclass_2$
(namely $q_3$ and~$q_4$).
Nonetheless, 
we need to argue that for the machines produced by the
algorithm, the transition function is well-defined.

\begin{remark}
Before that, we pause to 
suggest where this well-definedness comes from with
two illustrations.
Consider again the machines from \cref{ex:FirstMinimization}.
Below we picture the connection between their states as a projection,
$p(q_0)=r_0$, 
$p(q_1)=p(q_2)=r_1$,
$p(q_3)=p(q_4)=r_2$, 
and $p(q_5)=r_3$.
When the algorithm collapses $q_2$ and~$q_1$ into the 
single~$r_1$, it ensures that the two arrows from~$q_0$ to those redundant 
states become the single $\str{a},\str{b}$ arrow from~$r_0$ to~$r_1$.
We say that the transformation from $\FSM_I$ to $\FSM_O$ \definend{respects}
the starting machine's operation.
\begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{rc}
    \tabulartext{Input $\FSM_I$:}
      &\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min03.pdf}} \\[6.0ex]
    \tabulartext{Output $\FSM_O$:}
      &\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min04.pdf}}
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}

The other illustration uses tables.
% The map associating states in $\FSM_I$ with states in $\FSM_O$
% groups the $q$'s into $r$'s.
% Again, 
Moore's algorithm combines the states such that 
the transitions in $\FSM_O$ fit with\Dash that is, respect\Dash
the transitions in $\FSM_I$.
By this we mean that, for instance, $q_1\mapsto r_1$ and when presented with
input~\str{a} those transition to $q_3$ and~$r_2$, 
and the map respects the transition because $q_3\mapsto r_2$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{$\Delta_{\FSM_I}$}
    &\str{a}  &\str{b}  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  $q_0$  &$q_1$  &$q_2$  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  $q_1$  &$q_3$  &$q_4$ \\ 
  $q_2$  &$q_4$  &$q_3$ \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  $q_3$  &$q_5$  &$q_5$  \\ 
  $q_4$  &$q_5$  &$q_5$ \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  $q_5$  &$q_5$  &$q_5$  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\qquad
\begin{tabular}{c|cc}
  \multicolumn{1}{c}{$\Delta_{\FSM_O}$}
    &\str{a}  &\str{b}  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{1}{*}{$r_0$} &$r_1$ &$r_1$  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{2}{*}{$r_1$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$}  \\ 
  \\
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{2}{*}{$r_2$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{2}{*}{$r_3$}  \\
  \\ 
  \cline{2-3}
  \multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$} &\multirow{1}{*}{$r_3$}  \\ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\citetext{In a formula}{%
  The technical term is that $p$ is a homomorphism between
  the automata.}, 
the map~$\map{p}{\FSM_I}{\FSM_O}$
satisfies that 
$\Delta_{\FSM_O}(p(q),x)=p(\,\Delta_{\FSM_I}(q,x)\,)$ for all
$q\in\FSM_I$ and $x\in\Sigma$.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}
Moore's algorithm 
returns a machine that recognizes the same language as the input machine,
$\lang(\FSM_O)=\lang(\FSM_I)$, and 
that from among all of the machine
recognizing the same language has the minimal number of states. 
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We first argue that the algorithm halts for all input machines.
This is easy:~the algorithm halts at a step when no class splits and
since these machines have only finitely many states,
that step must eventually appear.

We next argue that $\FSM_O$'s transition function is well defined.
Simply, if there is a $\sim_n$ class where some
character sends two states in that class to different $\sim_n$~classes then
the algorithm does not terminate at that step. 
Instead it splits that class and goes to the next step.
So the algorithm cannot end with an ill-defined~$\Delta$. 

Next we verify that $\FSM_I$ and $\FSM_O$ recognize the same language. 
The input machine $\FSM_I$ starts in~$q_0$ while the output machine $\FSM_O$ 
starts in a state $r_0=\eclass_0$ that contains~$q_0$.
Feed both machines the same string, $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\!$. 
The first character of~$\sigma$ moves~$\FSM_I$ from $q_0$ to some state~$q_{i_1}$
and moves~$\FSM_O$ from a state that contains~$q_0$ 
to a state that contains~$q_{i_1}$.
The second character of~$\sigma$ moves~$\FSM_I$ from $q_{i_1}$
to some~$q_{i_2}$
and moves~$\FSM_O$ from a state that contains $q_{i_1}$ 
to a state that contains~$q_{i_2}$.
The machines 
proceed in this way until the string runs out.
At the end $\FSM_I$ is in a final state if and only if 
$\FSM_O$ is in a state that contains that final state, which is itself
a final state of~$\FSM_O$.
Thus the two machines accept the same set of strings.

\citetext{The last verification is that $\FSM_O$ has a minimal number of states}{%
  This is derived from the presentation in \autocite{HopcroftUllman}.
}.
Let~$\hat{\FSM}$ recognize
the same language as~$\FSM_O$;
we will show that it has at least as many states. 
We will do this by associating
each state in~$\FSM_O$ with at least one state 
in~$\hat{\FSM}$ in such a way 
that never are different states in~$\FSM_O$ associated
with the same state in~$\hat{\FSM}$.

Consider the union of the sets of states of the two machines 
(assume that they have no states in common, or else rename).
As we did with Moore's algorithm, 
begin by saying that two states in the union are
$0$-indistinguishable if both are final in their own machine 
or if neither is final.
Step~$n+1$ of this process starts with
the $\sim_n$~classes 
$\eclass_{n,0},\ldots{}\,\eclass_{n,k}$ of states from the union.
For each such class $\eclass_{n,i}$, see if it splits.
That is, see if there 
are two states in $\eclass_{n,i}$ that are sent by a character
$x\in\Sigma$ to different $\sim_n$~classes. 
If so then this gives the $\sim_{n+1}$ classes. 
When we reach a step with no splitting 
then we know which states are indistinguishable and
these form the $\sim$~classes.

Because $\lang(\FSM_O)=\lang(\hat{\FSM})$, 
the start states in the two machines are indistinguishable
and are in the same $\sim$~class.
Also, if two states are indistinguishable then their successor states
on any one input symbol $x\in\Sigma$ are also indistinguishable
from each other,
since if a string~$\sigma$ distinguishes between the successors 
then $\sigma\concat x$
distinguishes between the original two states.
In turn, the successors of these successors are indistinguishable, etc.

We are ready to define the association:~states 
in~$\FSM_O$ and $\hat{\FSM}$ are associated
if they are in the same $\sim$~class.
We first show that every state~$q$ of $\FSM_O$ is associated with 
at least one state of~$\hat{\FSM}$.
Because~$\FSM_O$ is the output of the minimization process, it has
no inaccessible state.
So there is a string
that takes the start state of~$\FSM_O$ to~$q$.
This string takes the start state of~$\hat{\FSM}$ to some~$\hat{q}$, and the 
prior paragraph applies to give that $q\sim\hat{q}$.

We finish by showing that there cannot be 
two different states of~$\FSM_O$ that are both associated with the same 
state of~$\hat{\FSM}$. 
If there were two such states then by \cref{lemma:StateEquivIsEquivRel} they
would be indistinguishable from each other.
But that's impossible because $\FSM_O$ is the output of the minimization
process, which ensures that all of its states are distinguishable from each
other.
\end{proof}

In this chapter we have seen that when we have a problem to solve
with a Finite State machine, 
often a nondeterministic machine
is easier and more natural.
An example is the algorithm that inputs a regular expression
and outputs a machine recognizing that expression.

But our algorithm for converting a nondeterministic machine 
to a deterministic machine has a problem.
Where the nondeterministic machine has $n$~states, 
the deterministic machine has $2^n$.

This section's result alleviates that.
We now have a three-step process:~from 
a problem we start with a nondeterministic
answer, convert that to an equivalent deterministic machine,
and then minimize to get a reasonably-sized final answer.
In practice, this gives good results.

\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
From the triangular table find the $\sim_i$ classes.
\begin{center}\small
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
  Pairs that are not $1$-distinguishable are: $q_0,q_1$, and $q_2,q_2$,
  and~$q_1,q_2$, so one class is $\eclass_{i,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$.
  Besides that, another pair of states that are not $1$-distinguishable
  is $q_3,q_5$ so another class is $\eclass_{i,1}=\set{q_3,q_5}$.
  The last class is $\eclass_{i,2}=\set{q_4}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
From the $\sim_i$~classes find the associated triangular table.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\eclass_{i,0}=\set{q_0,q_1}$,
  $\eclass_{i,1}=\set{q_2}$,
  and $\eclass_{i,2}=\set{q_3,q_4}$, 
\item $\eclass_{i,0}=\set{q_0}$,
  $\eclass_{i,1}=\set{q_1,q_2,q_4}$,
  and $\eclass_{i,2}=\set{q_3}$, 
\item $\eclass_{i,0}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_5}$,
  $\eclass_{i,1}=\set{q_2,q_3}$,
  and $\eclass_{i,2}=\set{q_4}$, 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}

$q_0$ from $q_2$, $q_3$, and~$q_4$.
So looking down the column headed by~$0$ we see checkmarks in rows
labeled $2$, $3$, and $4$.
The other columns work the same way.

        &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &    \\

\begin{display}\small

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that
$\eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2, q_5}$        
and
$\eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_3, q_4}$, and
from the machine you compute this table.
\begin{center} \small
  \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
    \cline{2-5}
    $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_2,q_3$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_2,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    $q_1,q_5$  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_3,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    $q_2,q_5$  &$q_2,q_5$                    &$q_4,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    \cline{2-5}
    $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Which lines of the table do you checkmark?
\item Give the resulting $\sim_1$~classes.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item These are the checkmarks.
\begin{display}\small
  \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
    \cline{2-5}
    \splitclasses{$q_0,q_1$}  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_2,q_3$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \splitclasses{$q_0,q_2$}  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_2,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \splitclasses{$q_1,q_5$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_3,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    \splitclasses{$q_2,q_5$}  &$q_2,q_5$                    &$q_4,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    \cline{2-5}
    $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
           &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
\end{display}
For example, the first row is checkmarked because on the right side the
second entry, the~\str{b} entry, has two classes that differ,
$\eclass_{0.0}\neq\eclass_{0,1}$.
\item The checkmarks show that we can $1$-distinguish 
$q_0$ from $q_1$ and~$q_2$ but not from~$q_5$.
And, we can tell $q_1$ and~$q_2$ from~$q_5$ but not from each other.
The matching $\sim_1$~classes are
$\eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_5}$, 
$\eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}$,         
and
$\eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}$        
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
This machine accepts strings with an odd parity, 
with an odd number of~\str{1}'s.
Minimize it, using the algorithm described in this section.
Show your work.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min07.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
  First, all the states are reachable, by inspection.

  Start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{center} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{3}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}    \\
      \cline{1-2}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{center}
   The checkmark denote a pair of states that are $0$-distinguishable and the 
   blanks denote pairs of states that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   Here are the two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1}        
   \end{equation*}
  Next we see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_0,q_2$                    &$q_1,q_1$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The answer is that they don't split.
  The process stops and here are the two $\sim$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}=\eclass_0=r_0
     \qquad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}=\eclass_1=r_1        
   \end{equation*}
  Incorporating the arrow information from the original machine gives 
  this minimized one.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min08.pdf}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For many machines we can find the unreachable states by eye,
but there is an algorithm.
It inputs a machine~$\FSM$ and initializes the set of reachable states
to $R_0=\set{q_0}$.
For $n>0$, step~$n$ of the algorithm is:~for each
$q\in R_n$ find all states~$\hat{q}$ reachable from~$q$ in one transition
and add those to make~$R_{n+1}$.
That is, 
$R_{n+1}=R_n\cup\set{\hat{q}=\Delta_{\FSM}(q,x)\suchthat\text{$q\in R_n$ and $x\in\Sigma$}}$.
The algorithm stops when $R_k=R_{k+1}$ and the set of reachable states 
is $R=R_k$.
The unreachable states are the others, $Q-R$.

For each machine, perform this algorithm.
Show the steps.\\
\begin{exparts*}
\item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min11.pdf}}
\item \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min13.pdf}}
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
  \begin{exparts}
  \item These are the sets.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|l}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Step $n$}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R_n$}
    \\ \hline
    $0$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $1$  &$\set{q_0,q_1}$  \\
    $2$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  \\
    $3$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$  
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  Thus the set of reachable states is $R=\set{q_0,q_1,q_2}$.
  The set of unreachable states is $Q-R=\set{q_3}$.
  Here is the machine with only the reachable states.
  \begin{display}
    \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min12.pdf}}
  \end{display}
  \item These are the sets.
  \begin{display}
  \begin{tabular}{r|l}
    \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Step $n$}}
      &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R_n$}
    \\ \hline
    $0$  &$\set{q_0}$  \\
    $1$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3}$  \\
    $2$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4}$  \\  
    $3$  &$\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4}$  \\  
  \end{tabular}
  \end{display}
  The set of reachable states is $R=R_2=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4}$.
  The set of unreachable states is $Q-R=\set{q_2,q_5}$.
  \end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Perform the minimization algorithm 
on the machine with redundant states
at the start of this section, the one on the right in~($*$) on 
page~\pageref{fig:MachinesWithMinAndNoMinStates}.
\begin{answer}
  For convenience, here is that machine.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/fsa/fsa17.pdf}
  \end{display}
  Every state is reachable, by inspection.

  Start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
          &    &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There is a checkmark in box $i,j$ if the states $q_i$ and~$q_j$ 
   are $0$-distinguishable. 
   The blanks denote pairs that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   There are two $\sim_{0}$~equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_1,q_2,q_4}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_3, q_5}
   \end{equation*}

  The next step is to see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_1$}  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_2,q_3$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_2$}  &$q_1,q_3$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_4$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_2$}  &$q_1,q_3$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_4$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_2,q_4$  &$q_3,q_5$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_3,q_5$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck  &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  The only pairs of states that are $1$-distinguishable are
  $q_2,q_4$ and $q_3,q_5$.
  These are the $\sim_1$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2,q_4}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_3,q_5}        
   \end{equation*}

  Now try it again to see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_2,q_4$  &$q_3,q_5$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{1,3},\eclass_{1,3}$    &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_3,q_5$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{1,3},\eclass_{1,3}$    &$\eclass_{1,3},\eclass_{1,3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck  &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  The only pairs of states that are $1$-distinguishable are
  $q_2,q_4$ and $q_3,q_5$.
  These are the $\sim_1$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2,q_4}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_3,q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
  There is no splitting; these are the $\sim$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     r_0=\eclass_0=\eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \quad
     r_1=\eclass_1=\eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     r_2=\eclass_2=\eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_2,q_4}        
     \quad
     r_3=\eclass+3=\eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_3,q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
   Bringing in the transition information from the original machine
   gives this minimized version.
   \begin{display}
     \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min14.pdf}
   \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What happens when you minimize a machine that is already minimal?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Not a lot.
  You get back the original machine in that
  you get $r_0=\mathcal{E}_{n,0}=\set{q_0}$,
  $r_1=\mathcal{E}_{n,1}=\set{q_1}$, etc. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \url{https://www.eecs.wsu.edu/~cook/tcs/l10.html}
\end{credit}
This machine accepts strings described by \re{(ab|ba)*}.
Minimize it, using the algorithm of this section and
showing the work.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min09.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
  By inspection the states $q_6$ and~$q_7$ are unreachable, 
  so drop them.

  With that, start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &    &\ck  &  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &    &\ck  &  &  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   The checkmarks are for pairs of states that are $0$-distinguishable, and the 
   blanks denote pairs that are $0$-indistinguishable.
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}        
     \qquad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1, q_3, q_4, q_5}
   \end{equation*}
  Next we see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_3$}  &$q_4,q_0$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_4$}  &$q_4,q_4$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_5$}  &$q_4,q_2$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_3,q_4$}  &$q_0,q_4$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_0,q_2$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_4,q_5$}  &$q_4,q_2$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_3,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  So, $q_0$ and~$q_2$ are not $1$-distinguishable.
  On the other hand, as shown in the triangular table, 
  $q_1$ is $1$-distinguishable from $q_3$, $q_4$, and~$q_5$.
  And, $q_3$ is $1$-distinguishable from~$q_4$ but not from~$q_5$.
  Finally, $q_4$ is $1$-distinguishable from~$q_5$.
  These are the $\sim_1$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_3,q_5}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_4}        
   \end{equation*}

  Determine which states are $2$-distinguishable by
  computing whether any of those classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_5$  &$q_0,q_2$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{1,0},\eclass_{1,0}$    &$\eclass_{1,3},\eclass_{1,3}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_3,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,1}$    &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &  &     \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &  &     \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &      \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4} &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}        \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  There is no splitting so the minimal machine has four states.
   \begin{equation*}
     r_0=\eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0,q_2}
     \quad
     r_1=\eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     r_2=\eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_3,q_5}        
     \quad
     r_3=\eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_4}        
   \end{equation*}
  The transitions from the input machine give 
  this minimized one.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min10.pdf}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
If a machine's start state is accepting, must the minimized machine's
start state be accepting?
If you think ``yes'' then prove it, and if you think ``no'' then
give an example machine where it is false.
\begin{answer}\verified
  The answer is ``yes.''
  The initial state of the minimal machine, $r_0$, is the one that 
  contains~$q_0$.
  In the minimized machine a state is accepting if and only if it contains any 
  final states in the original machine.
  So if $q_0$ is accepting then $r_0$ is also accepting.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
Minimize this machine.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min01.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  So start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &      \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &      \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &    &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &       \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &     &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}       \\
      \cline{1-4}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_3}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_2, q_4}
   \end{equation*}

  Next we see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_1$}  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_3$}  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_2,q_2$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_2,q_4$}  &$q_1,q_4$                    &$q_4,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &      \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &      \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &       \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}       \\
      \cline{1-4}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  So, $q_1$ and~$q_3$ are not $1$-distinguishable but all the others are
  $1$-distinguishable.
  These are the $\sim_1$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1,q_3}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_4}        
   \end{equation*}

  Step~$2$ is to decide of any of the $\sim_1$~classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_3$}  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,2}$    &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &     \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &      \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &      \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck    &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &       \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}       \\
      \cline{1-4}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  So, $q_1$ and~$q_3$ are $2$-distinguishable.
  These are the $\sim_2$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_3}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,4} =\set{q_4}        
   \end{equation*}
   The starting machine is minimal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Minimize this.
Show the work, including producing the diagram of the minimized machine.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min17.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
  By eye, all of the states are reachable.
  So start with the triangular table, checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and~$q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &    &     &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2, q_5}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_3, q_4}
   \end{equation*}

  Next we see if we can $1$-distinguish any of these states that cannot
  be $0$-distinguished.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}

             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_2$}  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_2,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      $q_0,q_5$  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_2,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_5$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_3,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_2,q_5$}  &$q_2,q_5$                    &$q_4,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
\begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &\ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  These are the $\sim_1$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0,q_5}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}        
   \end{equation*}

  Iterate.
  See if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_5$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_2,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,0}$    &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,0}$  \\
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,1}$    &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{1,2},\eclass_{1,2}$    &$\eclass_{1,0},\eclass_{1,0}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &   &    \\

      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  The class $\eclass_{1,0}$ splits in two.
  The pairs that are not $2$-distinguishable are: $q_1,q_2$,
  and~$q_3,q_4$.
  These are the $\sim_2$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_5}        
   \end{equation*}

  Again.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1,q_2$                    &$q_3,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{2,1},\eclass_{2,1}$    &$\eclass_{2,2},\eclass_{2,2}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_3,q_4$  &$q_3,q_4$                    &$q_5,q_5$  
             &$\eclass_{2,2},\eclass_{2,2}$    &$\eclass_{2,3},\eclass_{2,3}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &   &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &   &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck   &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck  &\ck   &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}    \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  There is no splitting.
  These are the $\sim$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     r_0=\eclass_{3,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     r_1=\eclass_{3,1} =\set{q_1,q_2}        
     \quad
     r_2=\eclass_{3,2} =\set{q_3,q_4}        
     \quad
     r_3=\eclass_{3,3} =\set{q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
  Here is the minimized machine.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min18.pdf}
  \end{display}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
This machine has no accepting states.
Minimize it.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min15.pdf}
\end{center}
What happens to a machine where all states are accepting?
\begin{answer}
  We will go through the algorithm but before we do, observe that
  the answer must be that a machine with a minimal number of states
  and that accepts no string has one state, namely the initial state,
  and all arrows are loops.

  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  So start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{4}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &      \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &       \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &        \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}      \\
      \cline{1-3}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There is one $\sim_{0}$-equivalence class.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2, q_3}        
   \end{equation*}

  Next we see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{0}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{1}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_0,q_1$  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_2,q_3$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_0,q_2$  &$q_1,q_0$                    &$q_2,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_0,q_3$  &$q_1,q_0$                    &$q_2,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_1,q_2$  &$q_1,q_0$                    &$q_3,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_1,q_3$  &$q_1,q_0$                    &$q_3,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_2,q_3$  &$q_0,q_0$                    &$q_2,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{4}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &      \\
      \cline{1-1}
          &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &       \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &        \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}      \\
      \cline{1-3}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  Of course, if there is only one class then it cannot split, so 
  there is only one $\sim_1$ class.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_1, q_2, q_3}        
   \end{equation*}
  This is the resulting minimized machine, as predicted.
  \begin{display}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min16.pdf}
  \end{display}

  If a machine has that all states are accepting then a similar thing 
  happens.
  It recognizes the language $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$ and the
  minimal such machine has one state, which is both initial and accepting.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Minimize this machine.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min02.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  So start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
          &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
          &\ck &     &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &    &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
          &\ck &     &     &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}     \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
   \end{display}
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{0,0} =\set{q_0, q_2, q_3, q_5}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{0,1} =\set{q_1, q_4}
   \end{equation*}

  Next we see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_2$}  &$q_1,q_1$                    &$q_0,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_3$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_0,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_0,q_5$}  &$q_1,q_4$                    &$q_0,q_1$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_2,q_3$}  &$q_1,q_5$                    &$q_4,q_2$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,0}$  \\
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_1,q_4$                    &$q_4,q_1$  
             &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,1},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \splitclasses{$q_3,q_5$}  &$q_5,q_4$                    &$q_2,q_1$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      \splitclasses{$q_1,q_4$}  &$q_3,q_3$                    &$q_0,q_4$  
             &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,0}$    &$\eclass_{0,0},\eclass_{0,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck &     &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}     \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  So, $q_2$ and~$q_5$ are not $1$-distinguishable but all the other pairs are
  $1$-distinguishable.
  These are the $\sim_1$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{1,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,2} =\set{q_2,q_5}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,3} =\set{q_3}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{1,4} =\set{q_4}        
   \end{equation*}

  Finally we see if the one remaining multi-state classes splits.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}} 
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}  \\
      \cline{2-5}
      $q_2,q_5$  &$q_1,q_4$                    &$q_4,q_1$  
             &$\eclass_{1,1},\eclass_{1,4}$    &$\eclass_{1,4},\eclass_{1,1}$  \\
    \end{tabular}
    \hspace{3em}
    \begin{minimizetable}{6}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &   &  &  &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      \ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &  &  &   &   \\
      \cline{2-2}
      \ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2} &  &   &   \\
      \cline{3-3}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3} &  &     \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}  &       \\
      \cline{5-5}
      \ck &\ck &\ck  &\ck  &\ck &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{5}     \\
      \cline{1-5}
    \end{minimizetable}
  \end{display}
  Thus all of the states are not $2$-distinguishable.
  These are the $\sim_2$ classes.
   \begin{equation*}
     \eclass_{2,0} =\set{q_0}
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,1} =\set{q_1}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,2} =\set{q_2}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,3} =\set{q_3}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,4} =\set{q_4}        
     \quad
     \eclass_{2,5} =\set{q_5}        
   \end{equation*}
   The original machine is minimal.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  What happens if you perform the minimization procedure in
  \cref{ex:SecondMinimization} without first omitting the
  unreachable state?
\begin{answer}
  You will get a machine that recognizes the same language.
  The reachable states of this machine will form a minimal set.
  But there will still be unreachable states (although perhaps
  fewer of them.)  
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:MachineTakesNStepsToMinimize}
  Minimize.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{automata/asy/min/min06.pdf}
  \end{center}
  Note that the algorithm takes, roughly, a number of steps that
  are equal to the number of states in the machine.
\begin{answer}
  By inspection all of the states are reachable.
  So start by checkmarking the $i,j$~entries
  where one of $q_i$ and $q_j$ is accepting while the other is not.
  \begin{display} 
    \begin{minimizetable}{5}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{0}   &    &    &    &    \\
      \cline{1-1}
      &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{1}   &    &    &    \\
      \cline{2-2}
      &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{2}   &    &    \\
      \cline{3-3}
      &    &    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{3}   &    \\
      \cline{4-4}
      \ck &\ck &\ck &\ck    &\multicolumn{1}{|c}{4}   \\
      \cline{1-4}

   \end{display}
   There are two $\sim_{0}$-equivalence classes.
   \begin{equation*}
      \eclass_{0,0} =\set{0,1,2,3} 
      \quad
      \eclass_{0,1} =\set{4}
   \end{equation*}

  Next we see if any of these classes split.
  \begin{display} \small
    \begin{tabular}{r|ll|ll}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{a}}
                  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\str{b}}
